292 Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Comprehensive Education Planning (CEP) is a structured, data-driven process that guides schools in assessing their needs, identifying areas for growth, setting strategic goals and implementing targeted actions to improve student outcomes. Collaboratively developed by the School Leadership Team (SLT) in accordance with the requirements of Chancellor’s Regulation A-655, the CEP meets New York State Education Department (NYSED) requirements under the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) and aligns with NYC Public Schools’ (NYCPS) system-wide priorities. By engaging all stakeholders—school leaders, teachers, families, students and community members—schools can create a data-informed, equity- focused roadmap for student success and continuous improvement. Schools should focus their CEP on the five system-wide priorities below, connecting their planning to the data discussions and conversations with stakeholders held as part of the school’s needs assessment: Priority 1: All students learn to read well Priority 2: All students are physically and emotionally safe Priority 3: All students have a high-quality academic experience Priority 4: All students graduate college and career ready and have a strong plan and pathway to economic security Priority 5: All districts and schools are more inclusive and responsive for parents and families, including having more families choose NYC Public Schools The Education Planning Process The CEP follows an equity-driven, continuous improvement cycle, rooted in the system-wide priorities. This begins with a comprehensive needs assessment of the school’s most current data to identify priority needs and root causes. Next, the SLT establishes specific, measurable, attainable, relevant, and timely— SMART goal(s) aligned to each priority and determines targets to measure progress toward meeting the annual goals. The SLT and instructional leaders define key evidence-based key strategies, professional learning activities and parent, family and community empowerment efforts. They monitor the implementation of those strategies and activities and track progress through measurable targets. Then, the SLT aligns the school-based budget with human and instructional resources to support action plans. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 5 of 292 As schools set goals, develop key strategies, and plan action steps, they provide opportunities for all children to meet NYSED’s proficient and advanced levels of student academic achievement. These include evidence based instructional strategies that address the educational needs of historically underserved populations and tackle structural inequities. Schools indicate how the plan addresses the needs of Multilingual/English Language Learners (ML/ELL) through development of the school’s Language Allocation Policy (LAP) & Title III Application (if applicable) and the Language Translation and Interpretation (LTI) Plan, that are required as part of the CEP. Schools also address the needs of Students with Disabilities (SWD) through the High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities section of the plan. SLTs also develop a plan to improve attendance and reduce chronic absenteeism. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 6 of 292 Guidance on Conducting a Comprehensive Needs Assessment Directions: Conduct a comprehensive needs assessment for each Priority. The information below provides guidance for reviewing and analyzing student and school performance trends to uncover root causes for low student achievement. 1. Review Multiple Data Sources: Use the most current and available information including, but not limited to, the following sources of formative and summative data, as applicable: Qualitative and quantitative data, including enrollment and class size trends 2024-25 ESSA Accountability Data Instructional Leadership Framework (ILF), Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) Inventories, and NYC School Survey results Quality Review Reports, NYSED and NYCPS CSI or A/TSI Support Visits, Receivership Quarterly Reports, and Diagnostic Tool for School and District Effectiveness Framework (DTSDE) NYC School Quality Guide, Insight, New Visions, NWEA MAP and/or other formative assessment data, IEP Review Survey Data, Progress on IEP Goals Data, Program (SESIS-STARS Linkage) Reports, Mandated (Related) Services Reports, and NYCPS Tools for Understanding Your Data for student subgroup information 2. Analyze Data Trend(s) to Identify Areas of Strength and Need: Identify data trends by grade, cohort, and content area(s) to surface gaps and inequities. Compare data sets to identify instructional and school practices that are impacting student performance. Guiding questions to consider: What does the data reveal about improving and declining student performance trends within grades, cohorts, and student subgroups? What inequities are evident between highest and lowest performing student subgroups? Which subgroup(s) scored at Level 1 based on NYSED ESSA accountability indicators? What does the data suggest regarding the effectiveness (or ineffectiveness) of current school programs, practices, and/or strategies/initiatives? What areas may need to be strengthened? Are there any differences in student performance between program delivery models for ML/ELL and SWD? (e.g., for special education: special class, ICT, SETSS, related services only; for ML/ELL: Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) vs. Dual Language, etc.) How does delivery of instruction factor into data trends? What are the implications for teacher quality and professional learning? What inequities in special education referral and school disciplinary practices (e.g. suspensions, removals) are evident between student subgroups with IEPs? 3. Identify Root Cause(s): After analyzing the data, engage in a process for uncovering root causes or contributing factors for low student and school performance. Develop theories or hypotheses to explain DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 7 of 292 why these needs exist and why your school is getting its current outcomes. Root causes could be related to gaps and inequities in areas such as curriculum & instruction, school systems & structures, student support services, school climate & safety, and family & community engagement. Focus on the causes, rather than the symptoms. Use the 5 Whys Method or other methods for identifying root causes. Consider the questions below: What theories or hypotheses explain the current student and school outcomes? Are there root causes specific to certain subgroups, grades, cohorts, or content area(s)? Additional resources and supports are available below. Use this CEP Needs Assessment Worksheet to help your SLT and stakeholders identify appropriate data sources, data trends, root causes and priority needs. For guidance and best practices on engaging stakeholders in a comprehensive needs assessment and root cause analysis, see this deck. A current list of all CEP/Title I resources can be found on the iPlan Portal Public Resources Page. Provide your school’s mission statement: To create a community where students, parents, and staff work together to inspire leaders who innovate, own their learning, and empower one another so we can be our best selves. Develop a narrative summary that includes contextual information about your school’s community and its unique/important characteristics and partnerships: Include demographic information about your school’s student population. PS 14 The Fairview is located at 107-01 Otis Avenue and 108th Street in the Heart of Corona in District 24, New York City. P.S. 14 is currently home to 873 kindergarten through fifth-grade students, of whom 92% are Hispanic or Latino, 3% Asian or Native Hawaiian/ Other Pacific Islander, 2% White, 1% Black or African American, 2% American Indian or Alaska Native, and less than 1% Multiracial. Among the school’s population, 53% are English Language Learners and 19% are Students with Disabilities. 27% of students are in temporary housing. PS 14 was founded in 1914. We have deep connections to Corona, Queens, rich with history and tradition. Do you know why our school is named “The Fairview School”? It’s because we had a view of the World’s Fairs in 1939 and 1964. The ’39 theme was “The World of Tomorrow”, and the ’64 theme was "Peace through Understanding," dedicated to "Man's Achievement on a Shrinking Globe in an Expanding Universe". The PS 14 mission is to create a community where students, parents, and staff work together to inspire leaders who innovate, own their learning, and empower one another so we can be our best selves. At PS 14, we believe that all children have talents and strengths. We strive to engage all students in enriching and inspiring ways. All students take at least one semester of the following courses EVERY year: dance, theater, music/piano, engineering, computer science 4 all, and SEL (social/emotional learning through visual arts). We believe in kindness and courage, and we believe that adults and children alike are ultimately responsible for our own learning, growth, and improvement. As a Leader in Me school, we practice the 8 Habits of Highly Effective People with a goal of “owning our own learning.” Students have voice and choice in their learning, and we partner with parents — the people who know their children best — to best support all students. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 38 of 292 Priority 2 Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): As a United Community School, parents, teachers, staff members, and student representatives participate as members of our United Community School Advisory Board. The board meets monthly. We will add an agenda item to address students' educational, social, emotional, and health needs by connecting the school and its community resources to improve student success in the classroom and beyond. In addition to monthly Advisory Board Meetings, UCS holds a yearly school stakeholder forum. This forum is an opportunity for parents, teachers, and administrators to share ideas and give input on vital school-based programs—like our in-school dental services and other wraparound supports that help our students thrive. We will add this CEP goal as an action item to that event. At P.S. 14, students are engaged in decision-making processes as well. We have a Student Lighthouse Team composed of students from each of our 4th and 5th grade classes that meet weekly to discuss school events and issues. Additionally, students from all classes are engaged in decision-making through the use of Google Surveys completed during school-wide morning announcements. This year, students will also engage in Participatory Budgeting through Civics for All. At P.S. 14, we firmly believe in a restorative approach to discipline and maintaining accountability for our actions. (a) Our PBIS system, R.O.A.R. is at the heart of our restorative approach to discipline. Students are provided with clear, consistent guidelines for appropriate behavior across all areas of the school building and all adults use common language to remind students of the expectations. Additionally, each class creates their own mission statement which helps to foster a sense of classroom community. Through the Tina’s Tiger Troop program, we will empower our fourth and fifth grade students to become leaders in our school community by volunteering time during their lunch period to work as Tina’s Tiger Tutor’s assisting peers in the lower grades with academic support, Tiger Teachers-in-Training supporting teachers with classroom organization tasks, ROARing Recyclers model good recycling habits for their peers, ROARing Recess Regulators assist our school aides during recess time and Tina’s Tour Guides are trained to help welcome new students to the P.S. 14 school community. (b) P.S. 14 follows the DOE’s ladder of intervention when handling student incidents. Low level infractions are handled by the classroom teacher through one-one-one discussions with students and parents regarding DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 39 of 292 indiscretions. If a student engages in a higher-level behavioral infraction, our deans and administrators provide an additional layer of support by conferencing with the student, conducting parent outreach, and working with the classroom teacher and/or guidance team to develop an individual behavior support plan for the student. Such plans have included conflict resolution, short-term counseling, daily check-ins, daily behavior monitoring with a chart, weekly game sessions with the principal as a reward for exhibiting desired behavior, etc. This year, if groups of students or classes have been impacted by similar behaviors, the deans may work with the teacher to implement restorative circles or co-teach lessons from the curriculum module Fostering Respect for All: Empowering Students to Move from Bystanders to Allies or from the Random Acts of Kindness, Kindness Counts curriculum with the classroom teacher. (c) All staff members have participated in a variety of Leader in Me Professional Development opportunities. New this year, staff will be provided an in-house refresher course on our PBIS system and the various supports available to students. Additionally, multiple staff members have participated in training such as Therapeutic Crisis Intervention, conflict resolution, and restorative circles. These staff members work with colleagues to support students. Through our partnership with Western Queens all staff have participated in professional development on trauma-informed student relationships and de-escalation strategies. Our new partnership with Brain Power Wellness will further support staff in promoting focus, mindfulness and emotional wellness. The Social Justice Anti- Racism PLC (year 2) will continue to build additional staff learning opportunities. Human Resources: SIT members, Special Education PLC members Instructional Resources PD sessions for Quality IEP writing and Specially Designed Instruction Schedule Adjustments coverage for SIT meetings as needed Other Resources Needed DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 110 of 292 Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Section 6: Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) Academic Support NYCPS Multi-Tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) align with NYSED guidelines for providing Academic Intervention Services (AIS) to students who have not yet met state standards or are considered to be at- risk of not meeting state standards. The Multi-tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) framework, organizes supports into three tiers: Tier 1 provides schoolwide, high-quality programming for all students, including SWDs; Tier 2 offers data informed small-group interventions for at-promise students; and Tier 3 delivers intensive, individualized support for students with persistent needs. This facilitates the strategic planning and necessary resource alignment to ensure their school community appropriately and adequately serves all students well, in partnership with students and families. MTSS identifies the academic support services to be provided and ensures that students receive Response to Intervention (RtI) and Positive Behavioral Interventions and Support (PBIS) strategies as needed. See NYSED’s memo to view the criteria for determining AIS services. The Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) offers a wide range of evidence-based Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions and supports for student social-emotional, behavioral, physical and mental wellness, with particular attention to the unique needs of students in Temporary Housing and/or Foster Care, who may face additional challenges related to school stability, trauma and access to educational resources. A culturally responsive-sustaining approach to intervention embeds culturally relevant texts and historical narratives into literacy and social studies interventions, ensuring students receive the support that reflects their identities and lived experiences, increasing engagement and achievement. Use the charts below and a Multi-Tiered System of Support (MTSS) to match academic, social/emotional, behavioral and instructional resources with student needs to provide targeted support for all learners, especially at-risk students who experience difficulty meeting state standards, including those living in Temporary Housing and/or residing in Foster Care placements. Indicate specialized materials, technology- based tools and/or extra instruction, including Expanded Learning Time that supplement school-wide systems & supports and/or classroom level supports. Ensure that interventions address the unique social- emotional, behavioral and academic challenges faced by culturally and linguistically diverse students, as well as those impacted by housing instability and child welfare system involvement. There are 232 Students in Temporary Housing as of October 30, 2025. -221 are doubled up -10 are living in a shelter -1 is living in a hotel After a careful analysis of historical demographic, attendance and achievement data for your STH population, what trends do you notice, and how can these trends inform the services and support provided for this population? Analysis of our data shows that 232 students (27%) are in temporary housing. STH status is often correlated with higher absenteeism and academic gaps, making targeted supports essential. We use the annual STH Title I Survey to identify priorities; last year, 81 families ranked food/clothing, in-school academic help, and after-school tutoring as top needs. This year, we aim for at least 150 responses by October 31. Based on consistently strong demand and positive feedback, afterschool tutoring will again run three times per week with two providers. Survey input and program outcomes also guide enrichment: our Reading Club, piloted last year, will launch earlier (November 5, 2025) and include daily hot meals and quarterly family-focused celebrations. In addition, housing status is reviewed in weekly SIT and Attendance Team meetings to ensure appropriate supports are in place. To address food insecurity and strengthen community ties, we are also hosting a culturally inclusive Thanksgiving DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 115 of 292 dinner on November 25 for 250 participants, with take-home meals provided. Through this combination of data analysis, survey results, and ongoing review, we align services to directly meet the academic and social-emotional needs of our STH population. Describe the services you are planning to provide to the STH population. How were those services selected to best meet students’ needs? How will you (or did you) utilize the STH Title I Survey to select services aligned to need? Academic Support: Two Toolkit Tutors provide high-impact tutoring in groups of four or fewer, using supplemental mCLASS materials to strengthen foundational skills. Tutoring runs from September–June and includes both in-school sessions and our afterschool Reading Club. This year, the Reading Club will feature a session by author Ada Ari, who will share African folktales to encourage culturally responsive storytelling and literacy. Attendance Support: Because tutors build strong relationships with students, they are a valuable partner to families in addressing attendance challenges. Given that STH students are historically overrepresented among chronically absent students, this relationship-based approach is even more important. Social-Emotional Learning (SEL) Support: Starting November 2025, Mental Health Providers of Western Queens will lead 10-week group counseling sessions twice weekly during lunch, using the evidence-based SSET curriculum. Last year, 95% of participants in SSET were STH students. In addition, STH students receive priority access to our Article 31 clinician for weekly individual counseling. Basic Needs: Our weekly food pantry serves many STH families, who currently make up 40% of participants. All STH students also received uniforms at the start of the year, and coats will be distributed at our October 30 Parent Association meeting. Additional essentials—including lice kits, shoes, book bags, lunch boxes, and school supplies—are available year-round through our Family Center (Rm 219). Families are also supported in enrolling in T-Mobile’s Project 10Million, providing free internet hotspots for students. Monitoring & Ongoing Support: Monthly reports of students in temporary housing or foster care are shared with the Guidance Team, who conduct outreach and connect families to at-risk services. Housing status is also reviewed at Attendance and SIT meetings to ensure STH students are prioritized for timely supports. Service Selection: These services were selected based on analysis of attendance and achievement trends, input from the STH Title I Survey, and feedback from families. Academic support, basic needs, and SEL programming consistently emerge as top priorities, and program adjustments each year are guided by both survey results and program outcomes. The School-Parent Compact (SPC) is a component of the Parent and Family Engagement Policy (PFEP) that outlines how parents, the entire school staff, and students will share this responsibility. The activities and/or strategies included in your school's Parent and Family Engagement Policy should align with current Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) goals for improving student achievement and should be developed or updated with input and feedback from parent leaders and the Parent Coordinator. In addition, if the school community will be engaged in central parent engagement initiatives, such as Parent Academy, which will provide training for school communities to help strengthen family-school partnerships, please be sure to include these activities in the school's policy. In Title I schools, the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) represents the Title I parent community in supporting parent and family engagement. The Title I PAC Chairperson will serve in a consultative role with the SLT representing the school’s Title I parents. Schools are encouraged to use the sample Parent and Family Engagement and SPC templates below (which meet federal Title I parent and family engagement requirements) as guidance for updating the school's current policy. Parent and Family Engagement Policy Educational research shows a positive correlation between effective parental engagement and student achievement. The overall aim of this policy is to develop a parent and family engagement policy that will establish expectations and objectives for meaningful parent and family engagement in the school community. [School Name], in compliance with Section 1116 of Title I, Part A of the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), is responsible for creating and implementing a parent and family engagement policy to strengthen the connection and support of student achievement between the school and the families. The school's policy is designed to keep parents informed by actively involving them in planning and decision-making in support of the education of their children. Parents are encouraged to actively participate on the School Leadership Team, Parent Association (or Parent-Teacher Association), and Title I Parent Advisory Council as trained volunteers and welcomed members of the school community. The school community, with the meaningful involvement of parents and family members, will conduct an annual evaluation of the content and effectiveness of this parent and family engagement policy with Title I parents to improve the academic quality of the school. This information will be maintained by the school. Staff and parents of students participating in activities and programs funded by Title I, agree that this Compact outlines how parents, the entire school staff and students will share responsibility for improved academic achievement and the means by which a school-parent partnership will be developed to ensure that all children achieve State Standards on assessments. I. School Responsibilities: High Quality CurriculumI. School Responsibilities: High Quality Curriculum Provide high quality curriculum and instruction consistent with State Standards to enable participating children to meet the State’s Standards and Assessments by: • using academic learning time efficiently; • respecting cultural, racial and ethnic differences; • implementing a curriculum aligned to the State Learning Standards; • offering high quality instruction in all content areas; • providing instruction by highly qualified teachers and when this does not occur, notifying parents as required by the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA); I. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 124 of 292 Title I Program Information Title I Program Information Part 1: Title I Program Type Title I Requirements and Strengthening Title I Parent Involvement: Under USDOE’s Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), Title I schools are required to address professional learning and parent and family engagement in the Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP). To strengthen parent leadership in Title I schools, and increase the involvement of all parents of Title I eligible children in improving students’ academic outcomes, all Title I schools are required to establish a Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) that will serve as the consultative and representative body for all Title I parents in the school to provide voice into the spending of Title I funds. The SLT is responsible for facilitating consultation with the Title I Parent Advisory Council regarding the joint development of the CEP, Parent and Family Engagement Policy, School Parent Compact (SPC) and the use of the 1% parent involvement set-aside in Title I schools. The Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) Chairperson (or alternate) is expected to attend all regular meetings of the School Leadership Team (SLT). Title I parents, as represented by the Title I PAC Chairperson, must be consulted regarding the use of the Title I 1% set-aside for parent and family engagement. The Title I 1% set-aside funding is used to supplement parent and family engagement activities as described in the school’s Parent and Family Engagement Policy and must be aligned with student achievement goals as outlined in the CEP. For additional guidance on the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC), go to the Protocols and Timeline for Title I Parent and Family Engagement Activities document. If a required component is addressed elsewhere in this plan, you may refer to the section or Priority where the response can be found. For additional information, visit the Title I Program Description (US Department of Education). To be eligible for the flexibility consolidation of Federal funds, a Schoolwide Program school must identify in its Schoolwide plan (CEP) which programs are included in its consolidation and the amount each program contributes to the consolidated Schoolwide pool. Additionally, the school plan must document that it has met the intent and purposes of each program whose funds are consolidated2. Explanation/Background: Title I Schoolwide Program schools are expected to use the flexibility available to them to integrate services and programs with the aim of enhancing the entire educational program and helping all students reach proficient and advanced levels of achievement. In addition to coordinating and integrating services, Schoolwide Program schools may combine most Federal, State and local funds to provide those services. By consolidating funds from Federal, State, and local sources, a Schoolwide Program school can address its needs using all its available resources. This gives a school more flexibility in how it uses available resources to meet the identified needs of all its students. Consolidating funds in a Schoolwide Program means that a school treats the funds it is consolidating like they are a single “pool” of funds. In other words, the funds from the contributing programs in the school lose their individual identity and the school has one flexible pool of funds. The school uses funds from this consolidated Schoolwide pool to support any activity of the Schoolwide Program without regard to which program contributed the specific funds used for a particular activity – so long as the use of funds meets the intent and purposes of the programs from which the funds originate. To consolidate funding in a Schoolwide Program, the school does not literally need to combine funds in a single account or pool with its own accounting code. Rather, the word “pool” is used conceptually to convey that a Schoolwide Program school has the use of all consolidated funds available to it for the dedicated function of operating a Schoolwide Program without regard to the identity of those funds. Consolidating Federal funds in a Schoolwide Program has the following additional advantages: Consolidating Federal funds eases the requirements for accounting for funds from each specific program separately, because a Schoolwide school is not required to distinguish among funds received from different sources when accounting for their use. A school that consolidates Federal funds in its Schoolwide Program is not required to meet most of the statutory and regulatory requirements of the specific Federal programs included in the consolidation (e.g., semi-annual time and effort reporting for Title I). However, the school must ensure that it meets the intent and purposes of the Federal programs included in the consolidation so that the needs of the intended beneficiaries are met. 2. The intent and purposes of the Federal programs indicated on the chart above (Part 4c of this section) are as follows: Title I, Part A – Schoolwide Programs: To upgrade the entire educational program in the school in order to improve the academic achievement of all students, particularly the lowest-achieving students. This includes provision of services for Students in Temporary Housing (STH). Title I 1003(a), School Improvement: Comprehensive Support and Improvement (CSI), Additional Targeted Support and Improvement (ATSI) and Targeted Support and Improvement (TSI) Funding: DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 130 of 292 Support implementation of school improvement plans that aims to improve instruction and address the identified needs Title II, Part A: Supplementary funding to improve student academic achievement by reducing class size in grades K, 1, 2, and 3, with an emphasis on grades with an average register greater than 20. If space is not available to form additional classes, funds may support push-in teacher(s) to supplement the instructional program. For schools that choose to reduce class size, priority must be given to reducing class size in general education settings and must demonstrate the following: Reduce class sizes to 15-18 students or fewer (including the use of co-teaching and floating teachers that bring the student to teacher ratio to one teacher per 15-18 students); Be accompanied by a rigorous curriculum, effective teachers, and a sustained, job-embedded professional development plan. Title III, Part A: To help ensure that children with limited English proficiency become proficient in English, develop high academic attainment in English, and meet the same challenging State academic content and achievement standards in the core academic subjects that all other children are expected to meet. Another purpose of this program is to increase the capacity of schools to establish, implement and sustain high-quality language instruction programs and English language development programs that assist schools in effectively teaching students with limited English proficiency. Title III, Part A is also designed to promote the participation of parents and communities of limited English proficient children in English language instruction programs. Title III Immigrant: Supplementary and enhanced services to immigrant students, in the areas of English language acquisition and content area achievement. Immigrant children and youth are defined as individuals who are aged 3 through 21, have not been attending school in any one or more states for more than three (3) full academic years, and were not born in any state. “State” means the 50 states of the United States, the District of Columbia, and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. Children born to U.S. citizens abroad, the U.S. Virgin Islands, or any other U.S. territory that is not D.C. or Puerto Rico are to be considered immigrants. Title IV, Part A: Title IV funds are for schools that are among those with the greatest needs, have the highest numbers of students from low-income families, are identified for CS, ATSI or TSI, or are identified as persistently dangerous public schools. Allowable uses of funds under well-rounded education, safety and health, and educational technology include direct services for students, professional development for teachers and administrators, and supplemental educational resources. English as a New Language (ENL) Stand-alone ENL: Overview: English as a New Language (ENL) as per CR Part 154 ENL is a program designed to support English Language Learners (ELLs) in New York State as they acquire English language proficiency and meet the same academic standards as their native English-speaking peers. Commissioner's Regulation (CR) Part 154 outlines the requirements for ENL programs. Stand-Alone ENL: This model dedicates specific time for explicit English language instruction separate from core content areas. It allows for focused attention on language skills (listening, speaking, reading, writing) and provides targeted support for ELLs. Based on the outcome of the language proficiency data from the New York English as a second Language Achievement Test (NYSESLAT) and/ or the New York State Identification Test for English Language learners (NYSITELL), students are placed in grade level classes with the maximum amount of ENL instructional minutes needed to support their language level. -Entering (EN) leveled students receive 360 minutes per week with a total of 180 minutes as stand alone instruction. -Emerging (EM) leveled students also receive 360 minutes per week. They receive between180 minutes of stand alone instruction based on their needs. -Transitioning (TR) leveled students receive 180 minutes per week. ENL teachers work with ENL students within the student's classrooms in heterogeneous and homogeneous proficiency groups depending on the needs of the students. Common Branch Teachers who hold an ESOL license and have ELL students in their classrooms service their students the maximum number of instructional minutes they are entitled to using ENL methodology throughout the day in all content areas that support their ELL students, including time for stand alone instruction. All students in all classes are articulated during the previous school year with data to predict next NYSESLAT proficiency levels as well as ELA/Math State Exam scores. Students are also given summative assessments as per NYC DOE policy. All data is transferred as a temporary baseline for the following school year before a baseline is performed for the current grade. All classrooms are heterogeneously grouped however, some classes on each grade are assigned for 180 minutes of stand alone ENL Teacher support and other classes on each grade are assigned for 180 minutes of stand alone ENL Teacher support, depending on the level and needs of the ENL students. All ELL providers are push-in for stand-alone provide scaffolding, modification/adaptations based on language needs. ELL providers promote team teaching, station teaching, and parallel teaching based on lessons, topics, or units. Integrated ENL: English as a New Language (ENL) as per CR Part 154 ENL is a program designed to support English Language Learners (ELLs) in New York State as they acquire English language proficiency and meet the same academic standards as their native English-speaking peers. Commissioner's Regulation (CR) Part 154 outlines the requirements for ENL programs. Integrated ENL: This model involves incorporating English language development instruction within the context of core content areas (English Language Arts, Math, Science, Social Studies). It aims to provide meaningful and relevant language learning opportunities for students while they engage with academic content. Based on the outcome of the language proficiency data from the New York English as a second Language Achievement Test (NYSESLAT) and/ or the New York State Identification Test for English Language learners (NYSITELL), students are placed in grade level classes with the maximum amount of ENL instructional minutes needed to support their language level. -Entering (EN) leveled students receive 360 minutes per week with 180 minutes of integrated instruction. -Emerging (EM) leveled students receive 360 minutes per week with 180 minutes of integrated instruction. -Transitioning (TR) leveled students receive 180 minutes per week with up to 180 minutes of integrated instruction. -Expanding (EX) leveled students receive 180 minutes per week of integrated instruction in ELA or other core related content area. -Commanding (CM) leveled students receive 90 minutes per week of integrated instruction in ELA or other core content area. ENL teachers work with ENL students within the classrooms in heterogeneous and homogeneous proficiency groups depending on the needs of the students. Common Branch Teachers who hold an ESOL license and have ELL students in their classrooms use ENL methodology throughout the day in all content areas to support their ELL students. All classrooms are heterogeneously grouped however, some classes on each grade are assigned for up to 180 minutes of integrated ENL Teacher support and other classes on each grade are assigned for 180 minutes of integrated ENL Teacher support, depending on the level and needs of the ENL students. All students in all classes are articulated during the previous school year with data to predict next NYSESLAT proficiency levels as well as ELA/Math State Exam scores. Students are also given summative assessments as per NYC DOE policy. All data is transferred as a temporary baseline for the following school year before a baseline is performed for the current grade. Bilingual Education (If applicable) Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE): Transitional Spanish Bilingual Education Program: Students in the TBE program receive ENL services from their Bilingual licensed teacher as stated above. They also receive up to 2 core content subject areas in Spanish. Students at the Entering and Emerging language level receive the minimum of 2 core content area subjects in Spanish. Transitioning and Expanding language level students receive a minimum of 1 core content subject area in Spanish. TBE classes are scheduled to conduct a Native Language Arts 1 period a day where they integrate social studies and science into the lesson. TBE classes are heterogeneously grouped. Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) program TBE programs gradually increase English instruction, using ESL methodology, in academic content areas such as mathematics, science, and social studies. The percentage of the use of native language can vary depending on the student’s English proficiency level and the nature of the content area. For example, when teachers are planning mathematics activities using the balanced approach, each language should be given equal attention in planning lessons. For example, mathematical concepts might be taught using the native language; reinforcement of those concepts might be done in English. Mathematical terms must be developed in English and their contextual meaning clarified. Students need opportunities to use mathematical language and to discuss mathematical concepts in their own words in both the native language and English. Also, English language development is strengthened through participation in English-taught enrichment. TBE classes are formed in grades K-5 when there are 15 or more ELLs of the same language in two contiguous grades. Teachers using ENL methods to teach content should consider scaffolding instructional strategies. Grades Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE): Students in the Dual Language program receive all content subject areas in both English and Spanish on alternating days. The class is heterogeneously grouped, with at least 50% of students expected to have native proficiency in English and the remainder in Spanish, ensuring balanced peer language models. The Dual Language program is a developmental, language-enriched model that integrates English-dominant students with Spanish-dominant students. Through this structure, students build second-language proficiency while maintaining grade-level achievement in all subjects. The program pursues three central goals: Grade-level achievement: Students meet or exceed New York State and City standards through rigorous instruction in two languages. Bilingualism and biliteracy: Students graduate with strong skills in listening, speaking, reading, and writing in both English and Spanish. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 146 of 292 Socio-cultural competence: Students develop positive cross-cultural skills and perspectives, preparing them to thrive in a global society. Instruction is balanced across languages using second-language acquisition strategies and culturally responsive pedagogy. Language Arts are taught using Native Language Arts (NLA), ESL, and ELA components. Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura serves as the foundation for literacy, Envisions for mathematics, Passport for social studies, and Amplify for science—all delivered bilingually to ensure students have consistent access to academic content in both languages. By the end of the program, students are expected to demonstrate proficiency in comprehending, speaking, reading, and writing in both languages, transferring academic skills fluidly across contexts. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 147 of 292 2. How does your school ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes are provided according to students’ English language proficiency levels in each program model? ENL Program At P.S. 14 (24Q014), the English as a New Language (ENL) program is staffed by 24 certified ENL teachers, ensuring that all students receive their mandated instructional minutes with fidelity. Instruction is designed to target the four language modalities—speaking, reading, writing, and listening—while aligning to content standards. Students at the Entering (EN) and Emerging (EM) proficiency levels receive 360 minutes per week of integrated and stand-alone ENL instruction, embedded within content areas such as literacy, mathematics, science, and social studies. Students at the Transitioning (TR) and Expanding (EX) levels receive 180 minutes per week, while those at the Commanding (CM) level are provided with at least 90 minutes per week to support continued development and ensure smooth transition. Teachers employ scaffolding strategies—including visuals, sentence frames, modeling, and structured peer interactions—so that all students can engage in grade-level work while acquiring academic English. The capacity provided by 24 certified ENL teachers allows for flexible grouping, co-teaching in ICT and general education classrooms, and targeted small-group instruction that meets the diverse linguistic needs of students. Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) Program The TBE program at 24Q014 ensures that students receive instruction in their home language while gradually increasing English exposure to promote biliteracy and academic success. Instruction is balanced across languages using ESL, English Language Arts (ELA), and Native Language Arts (NLA) in the following allocations: Emerging: 60% Spanish / 40% English Transitioning: 50% Spanish / 50% English Expanding: 25% Spanish / 75% English At the emergent stage, 60% of instruction is conducted in the home language to secure content understanding and build confidence, while 40% is provided in English to begin developing academic vocabulary. As students progress through proficiency levels, English instruction gradually increases until students are prepared to learn fully in English. Certified ENL and bilingual teachers collaborate to ensure that home language literacy serves as a foundation for English acquisition, making the transition seamless and academically rigorous. Dual Language Program The Dual Language program follows an alternating day model, with instruction delivered in English one day and Spanish the next. This structure ensures that all students—English-dominant and Spanish-dominant alike—develop literacy and content knowledge in both languages. A 90-minute literacy block anchors the program, with components of reading, writing, and word study taught in both languages using a simultaneous literacy approach. Teachers leverage Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura for balanced literacy, Envisions for mathematics, Passport for social studies, and Amplify for science. Each curriculum is implemented bilingually, ensuring students not only meet mandated HLA minutes but also gain content mastery in both languages. Instruction is further supported by the school’s cadre of ENL-certified teachers, who co-plan and co-teach to ensure all learners thrive in a linguistically rich environment. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 148 of 292 3. How are ENL, English Language Arts (ELA) and if applicable, Home Language Arts (HLA) instructional minutes delivered in each program model? Dual Language (DL) Programs ENL: In the Dual Language program at 24Q014, ENL instruction is integrated into content areas taught in English and is supported by certified ENL teachers. For example, ENL strategies are embedded in Into Reading during English literacy instruction and in Envisions during math instruction. Students at Entering/Emerging levels may also receive additional stand-alone ENL support to meet their mandated minutes. These supports ensure that language development occurs simultaneously with content learning. ELA: English Language Arts is delivered through Into Reading, where students build reading, writing, speaking, and listening skills in English. Instruction is differentiated by proficiency level, with scaffolds such as leveled texts, sentence stems, and guided writing tasks for Entering and Emerging students, while Transitioning and Expanding students work with increasingly complex texts and writing assignments. HLA: Home Language Arts (HLA) is delivered through Arriba La Lectura in Spanish. Instruction builds Spanish literacy skills, which serve as a foundation for cross-language transfer to English. For example, vocabulary and comprehension strategies introduced in Spanish are reinforced in English literacy blocks. The alternating day model ensures a balance of English and Spanish instruction while meeting mandated HLA minutes. English as a New Language (ENL) Programs ENL: In the stand-alone ENL program, students receive direct, explicit instruction in English aligned to their proficiency levels. Entering/Emerging students receive 360 minutes per week, Transitioning/Expanding students 180 minutes, and Commanding students at least 90 minutes. Teachers use scaffolded instruction drawn from the Into Reading program, adapting lessons with visuals, graphic organizers, and interactive dialogue to build English proficiency while accessing grade-level standards. ELA: ELA is delivered in English using the Into Reading curriculum, which emphasizes phonics, fluency, comprehension, and writing development. ENL teachers co-plan with classroom teachers to ensure that academic vocabulary and reading strategies are reinforced during ELA blocks. This alignment allows students to practice English literacy skills in both ENL and ELA settings. HLA: HLA is not a component of the stand-alone ENL program at 24Q014, as the focus is exclusively on building English proficiency. Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) Programs ENL: In TBE classrooms, students receive stand-alone ENL instruction aligned to their mandated minutes and proficiency levels. ENL strategies are also integrated into bilingual content instruction. For example, ENL teachers support English language development during Amplify Science units by reinforcing key vocabulary and sentence structures in English while content knowledge is introduced in Spanish. ELA: ELA instruction is delivered in English using Into Reading. Lessons are carefully scaffolded to support students at varying proficiency levels. Entering/Emerging students may focus on oral language development and foundational reading skills, while Transitioning/Expanding students engage with more complex texts and written expression. HLA: Home Language Arts (HLA) is provided in Spanish through Arriba La Lectura. Instruction strengthens native language literacy, which directly supports English acquisition. Students transfer comprehension strategies, writing structures, and academic vocabulary from Spanish into English over time. The proportion of HLA decreases as DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 149 of 292 students advance proficiency levels: 60% Spanish/40% English at Emerging, 50%/50% at Transitioning, and 25%/75% at Expanding. What is the composition of the ELL subgroups at your school? What percentage of ELLs are Newcomers? How does effective instruction for Newcomers differ from those provided to Developing or Long-Term ELLs? At P.S. 014 Fairview (DBN 24Q014), the composition of English Language Learners (ELLs) reflects a population predominantly made up of students at the early stages of English acquisition. According to the Spring 2025 ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT), there are 563 active ELLs enrolled at the school. Of these, **541 students (83.5%)** are identified as **Newcomers**, defined as ELLs who have received services for 0 to 3 years. An additional **107 students (16.5%)** are classified as **Developing ELLs**, having received services for 4 to 6 years. Notably, **there are no Long-Term ELLs** (students with 7 or more years of service) enrolled at this time (EDAT, p. 4). This composition indicates that the vast majority of ELLs at P.S. 014 are still within their foundational phase of English language development, which has direct implications for instructional planning. **Effective instruction for Newcomers** must prioritize **basic interpersonal communication skills (BICS)** and **foundational literacy**. Instruction is most impactful when it includes rich oral language development, visual supports, home language scaffolds (e.g., bilingual glossaries, dual-language books), and strategic grouping that allows for peer modeling. Teachers should also integrate culturally responsive practices that validate students’ backgrounds and help them navigate new academic and social contexts. In contrast, **Developing ELLs**, who typically function at the Transitioning or Expanding proficiency levels, require instruction that bridges their existing language skills with **academic language proficiency (CALP)**. This includes targeted vocabulary instruction, explicit teaching of grammatical structures in context, and content-integrated language tasks. These students benefit from consistent opportunities to engage in extended discourse and structured writing that supports them in mastering complex language functions across content areas. While P.S. 014 currently does not serve any Long-Term ELLs, best practices for that subgroup include intensive, differentiated support in academic writing and reading comprehension, often involving targeted interventions and close collaboration with support staff such as ENL and special education teachers. In summary, the school’s ELL subgroup profile—dominated by Newcomers—demands that instruction be highly scaffolded, linguistically supportive, and culturally affirming to ensure that students build the foundational skills necessary for long-term academic success (EDAT, p. 4). DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 153 of 292 What percentage of ELLs are Students with Inconsistent/Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE)? How do you differentiate effective instruction for SIFE? According to the **Spring 2025 ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT)** for P.S. 014 Fairview, **2 out of 563 active ELLs** are identified as **Students with Inconsistent/Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE)**. This represents **0.3% of the total ELL population** at the school (EDAT, p. 4). While the proportion of SIFE students is relatively small, this subgroup requires **intensely differentiated and trauma-informed instruction** due to their unique educational histories. SIFE are students who have been in the U.S. for less than twelve months and are two or more years below grade level in literacy and/or math due to interrupted schooling in their home countries. **Effective instruction for SIFE** differs from typical ELL programming in the following ways: 1. **Literacy and Numeracy Rebuilding**: Instruction must integrate both **basic literacy** (phonics, decoding, sight word recognition) and **foundational math skills**, often delivered through small-group, multi-sensory methods. 2. **Extended Time and Acceleration**: SIFE students benefit from **extended instructional time**, whether through after-school support, double blocks for ENL, or dedicated interventions that address both language and content gaps. 3. **Home Language Resources**: Strategic use of **home language materials** and **bilingual staff** can support comprehension and affirm prior knowledge, especially when gaps in formal schooling exist. 4. **Culturally Responsive Trauma-Informed Supports**: SIFE students may have experienced displacement, trauma, or family separation. Effective instruction incorporates **SEL routines**, **trust-building strategies**, and **flexibility in expectations**. 5. **Content Scaffolding**: Teachers must simplify content without diluting rigor—using visuals, graphic organizers, sentence frames, and chunked texts—ensuring access to grade-level ideas even as students build language and academic stamina. 6. **Progress Monitoring and Flexibility**: Frequent, formative assessments are critical to adjusting instruction and measuring growth, particularly since traditional grade-level benchmarks may not apply linearly to SIFE. In sum, although only **0.3%** of P.S. 014's ELL population is classified as SIFE, addressing their needs requires a **highly intentional, interdisciplinary approach**. Collaboration between ENL teachers, interventionists, counselors, and families is essential to ensure equitable access to both academic and social-emotional learning opportunities (EDAT, p. 4). What percentage of ELLs are Long-Term ELLs? How does effective instruction for Long-Term ELLs differ from instruction designed for Developing ELLs? At P.S. 014 Fairview, **0% of current ELLs are classified as Long-Term ELLs (LTEs)**, according to the Spring 2025 ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT). This indicates that none of the 563 English Language Learners enrolled at the school have received ELL services for **7 or more years**, which is the threshold for long-term classification (EDAT, p. 4). While there are no LTEs currently enrolled, understanding the instructional distinctions between Long-Term and DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 154 of 292 Developing ELLs remains critical, especially for schools that may receive transfer students or are planning ahead for potential shifts in enrollment. **Developing ELLs**, those with 4 to 6 years of service, often function at **Transitioning or Expanding proficiency levels**. Effective instruction for this group focuses on **bridging foundational skills with more complex academic language**. Key instructional strategies include: * Explicit teaching of **academic vocabulary and syntax** across content areas; * **Scaffolded writing assignments** that guide students through extended responses; * Integration of **content-area literacy**, ensuring students can read, write, and discuss standards-based material in subjects like science and social studies; * Regular **formative assessments** to monitor progress toward exiting ENL services. In contrast, **Long-Term ELLs** typically demonstrate **stalled progress** in one or more language domains— often writing—and may exhibit a **disconnect between oral fluency and academic performance**. Their instruction must be: * **Intensified and targeted**, particularly in writing and reading comprehension; * Built on **analyzing language structures** explicitly, such as grammar, text structure, and transitions; * Reinforced through **frequent feedback loops**, conferencing, and revision-based assignments; * Supported through **language-rich environments** that demand high levels of academic discourse and argumentation; * Enhanced by **collaboration with content teachers** to align language objectives within curriculum standards. Moreover, LTEs benefit from **individualized learning plans**, mentorship, and possibly counseling support to address the social-emotional factors that may contribute to long-term language learner status, such as disengagement or repeated grade retention. In summary, while P.S. 014 currently has **no students classified as Long-Term ELLs (0.0%)**, effective instruction for this group—should they be enrolled in the future—would differ from Developing ELLs by placing stronger emphasis on **academic rigor, explicit language instruction, and progress recovery strategies** (EDAT, p. 4). DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 157 of 292 4. What are the home languages of ELLs with the largest representation at your school? What staff, structures, and processes does your school have to support communication and learning that values these home languages? At P.S. 014 Fairview, the linguistic profile of English Language Learners (ELLs) is overwhelmingly shaped by Spanish. According to the Spring 2025 ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT), Spanish is the home language of 550 out of 563 ELLs, accounting for 97.9 percent of the school’s multilingual population (EDAT, p. 7). This concentration has a profound impact on the school’s instructional programming, staffing design, and family engagement efforts. While other home languages—such as Arabic, Chinese, Nepali, Pilipino, and Ukrainian—are present, each represents five or fewer students. As a result, the school’s overall linguistic diversity exists on a small scale compared to the overwhelming majority of Spanish-speaking students and families. In response to this landscape, P.S. 014 has intentionally structured its programs to provide robust Spanish bilingual pathways. The May 2025 ELL Data Update Report (EDUR) confirms that the school operates Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) programs in grades K–5 for both general and special education settings, including ICT and 12:1:1 classrooms. In addition, Dual Language (DL) programs in grades K–3 bring together Spanish-speaking students with their English-proficient peers in a model that promotes biliteracy and biculturalism (EDUR, p. 3). Both program formats enable students to access rigorous content in their home language while gradually developing English proficiency, ensuring that language is a bridge rather than a barrier to academic achievement. To support this work, the school is staffed with bilingual-certified teachers and a team of ENL-certified teachers who provide both push-in and pull-out services. These educators collaborate to balance home language instruction with targeted English development, creating classrooms where students build biliteracy and transfer skills across languages. Additional support comes from bilingual paraprofessionals and family-facing staff who help maintain strong connections between the school and Spanish-speaking households. Language access for families remains a high priority. P.S. 014 utilizes the DOE’s Translation and Interpretation Unit and, as reported in the EDUR, received $7,007 in funding to support these services. At the time of the most recent update, 100 percent of these funds had been scheduled, with nearly 38 percent already spent, guaranteeing that essential school communications—from entitlement letters to report cards—are translated into Spanish and other home languages as necessary (EDUR, p. 9). The school also demonstrates strong compliance with foundational processes for identifying student language needs. With 99.4 percent compliance in administering the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) and 99.9 percent accuracy in home language coding in ATS, P.S. 014 ensures that students are properly identified and placed into the correct language service pathways (EDUR, p. 7). Equally important, the school fosters a sense of belonging among its Spanish-speaking families through bilingual orientations, translated entitlement letters, and culturally responsive events. The Parent Coordinator, supported by bilingual staff, plays a key role in outreach and engagement, ensuring families are not only informed but also active partners in their children’s education. Collectively, these structures create a linguistically and culturally responsive environment where Spanish-speaking ELLs and their families feel valued, supported, and positioned for success. 5. What are the racial and ethnic classifications of ELLs at your school? How are ELLs being included in planning and delivery of Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CR-SE)? At P.S. 014 Fairview, the racial and ethnic composition of the English Language Learner (ELL) population mirrors the multicultural character of the surrounding Queens community. According to the Spring 2025 ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT), the school enrolls 648 active ELLs, of whom 550 students (96 percent) identify as Hispanic. The remaining 26 students (4 percent) represent other groups, including 18 Asian students and 8 Black students, with fewer than DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 158 of 292 three students each identifying as Multiracial, White, American Indian, or Pacific Islander (EDAT, p. 9). This distribution confirms that P.S. 014’s ELL population is overwhelmingly Hispanic, with Spanish as the predominant home language (EDAT, p. 7), while still maintaining a smaller but important representation from other racial and cultural backgrounds. The school has responded to this demographic reality by embedding Culturally Responsive and Sustaining Education (CR-SE) across its instructional, cultural, and engagement practices. Central to this work is the recognition of language as a cultural asset. Through Dual Language and Transitional Bilingual Education programs in Spanish, students are supported in developing biliteracy and academic English while affirming their home language identity (EDUR, p. 3). Teachers use texts in both English and Spanish that reflect students’ lived experiences, histories, and cultural narratives, ensuring that literacy instruction is both rigorous and relevant. Representation is woven into content and school spaces alike. Instruction integrates literature, history, and themes from Latin American and Caribbean contexts, while classrooms and hallways highlight student heritage through bilingual bulletin boards, cultural displays, and libraries stocked with diverse texts. These intentional choices reinforce students’ cultural identities while fostering pride and belonging. Pedagogical practices further align with CR-SE principles. Teachers employ collaborative learning structures that encourage storytelling, discussion, and peer teaching, positioning students as knowledge holders. Projects often invite students to explore and share their family histories and traditions in bilingual formats, amplifying student voice and fostering cross-cultural understanding among peers. Family and community engagement are also deeply responsive to the linguistic and cultural profile of the school. With more than 96 percent of ELLs identifying as Hispanic, P.S. 014 hosts Spanish-language family workshops, cultural celebrations, and translated parent communications to ensure accessibility. The Parent Coordinator, together with bilingual staff, plays a pivotal role in bridging home and school, offering families consistent support and guidance that respects cultural practices and strengthens trust. Finally, teachers and staff engage in ongoing professional learning to sustain CR-SE practices. Training in implicit bias, culturally affirming pedagogy, and equitable language policies—often in partnership with the Office of Multilingual Learners and community organizations—ensures that instructional practices remain aligned with student needs. Educators are encouraged to reflect critically on grading practices, language use, and classroom interactions to ensure equity for ELLs across all proficiency levels. In summary, while the ELL population at P.S. 014 is predominantly Hispanic, the school has developed a comprehensive approach that values linguistic and cultural identity as central to academic success. Through bilingual programming, culturally relevant curricula, inclusive pedagogy, and intentional family engagement, ELLs are both supported in their language development and affirmed as cultural contributors within the school community. 6. At the same time, the narrowing percentage of Entering students shows that newcomers are being integrated effectively and moving forward with the right scaffolds. What are trends reflected in the NYSESLAT results of ELL subgroups such as ELLs with IEPs and SIFE? What do you think is impacting the results for these subgroups? While the overall data show historic gains in proficiency—with 61.1% of students now performing at Expanding or Commanding levels—these subgroups continue to demonstrate significant challenges, underscoring the need for targeted supports. Trends in NYSESLAT Performance for ELLs with IEPs Of the 648 total ELLs, 127 students (19.6%) are dually identified as having an IEP (EDAT, p. 4). Their performance on DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 162 of 292 the NYSESLAT indicates that a disproportionate number remain concentrated in the Entering and Emerging proficiency levels. This subgroup contributed notably to the 230 students (62.5%) who did not meet sufficient ELP progress benchmarks in 2024 (EDAT, p. 10). The data confirm that ELLs with IEPs face compounded demands: acquiring English while also navigating cognitive, academic, or emotional disabilities. While P.S. 014 has demonstrated strong compliance with ENL programming and has expanded co-teaching models, challenges persist in fully integrating language and special education services. For instance, gaps can emerge if ENL and special education staff are not jointly planning lessons that address both linguistic and disability-related needs. Ensuring consistent collaboration and differentiated, bilingual special education supports will be key to moving more of these students beyond the early proficiency stages. Trends in NYSESLAT Performance for SIFE Students Although only two students (0.3%) are currently identified as SIFE (EDAT, p. 4), their results highlight the unique challenges of this subgroup. Both students scored at the Entering level across most modalities, particularly Reading and Writing, which demand sustained academic exposure. Neither student met sufficient ELP growth benchmarks, underscoring the difficulty of catching up without targeted interventions. Several factors contribute to these outcomes, including: Gaps in foundational literacy skills due to disrupted schooling, Limited exposure to academic language in both English and the home language, and Socio-emotional and resettlement challenges, which may affect attendance and focus. Addressing these barriers requires intensive, specially designed programming. Key supports include increased instructional time focused on literacy acceleration, trauma-informed practices to support engagement, and biliteracy approaches that leverage the home language as a foundation for English development. Even with their small representation in the student population, the needs of SIFE students must remain a high instructional priority given their vulnerability to long-term underperformance. Conclusion The Fall 2025 EDAT shows that P.S. 014 has made historic progress overall, with record numbers of students reaching Expanding and Commanding proficiency. However, ELLs with IEPs and SIFE students remain disproportionately represented in the lower proficiency bands. These findings reinforce the importance of expanding integrated ENL-special education collaboration, ensuring access to bilingual special education resources, and developing intensive, trauma-informed programming for SIFE learners. With these targeted strategies, P.S. 014 can continue to close gaps for its most vulnerable multilingual learners while sustaining its momentum of success. 10.
