Tier 1 Core Instruction 1. How does your school provide ELLs with a high-quality academic experience that leads to deeper learning? Targeted Language Instruction: Integrated and Stand-Alone ENL Instruction: Provide both integrated ENL within content areas and dedicated ENL classes to address specific language skills and needs. Scaffolding and Differentiation: Tailor instruction to ELLs' language proficiency levels, offering support such as visual aids, sentence frames, and adapted texts. Explicit Vocabulary and Grammar Instruction: Teach academic vocabulary and grammar explicitly to build a strong foundation for comprehension and expression. Language-Rich Environment: Create a classroom environment where students have ample opportunities to use and practice English in meaningful contexts. Culturally Responsive Pedagogy: Valuing Cultural Backgrounds: Recognize and celebrate students' diverse cultural backgrounds and experiences. Building on Prior Knowledge: Connect new learning to students' existing knowledge and experiences. Incorporating Culturally Relevant Materials: Use texts, images, and resources that reflect students' cultures and interests. Creating a Safe and Inclusive Classroom: Foster a sense of belonging and community where all students feel valued and respected. Rigorous and Engaging Instruction: High Expectations: Set high expectations for all ELLs and believe in their ability to succeed. Challenging Curriculum: Provide access to rigorous academic content that promotes critical thinking, problem- solving, and creativity. Authentic Tasks: Engage students in authentic tasks that have real-world relevance and connect to their lives. Collaborative Learning: Encourage students to work together, share ideas, and learn from each other. Assessment and Feedback: DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 165 of 292 Multiple Measures: Use a variety of assessments to gauge students' progress, including formative and summative assessments, observations, and performance tasks. Formative Feedback: Provide timely and constructive feedback that helps students understand their strengths and areas for improvement. Goal Setting: Help students set realistic and achievable goals for their language development and academic growth. Family and Community Engagement: Communication: Establish clear and open communication channels with families to share information about their child's progress and provide resources for supporting their learning at home. Partnerships: Partner with community organizations to provide additional resources and support for ELLs and their families. Professional Development: Culturally Responsive Teaching: Provide ongoing professional development for teachers on culturally responsive teaching practices, language acquisition, and differentiation strategies. Data-Driven Instruction: Equip teachers with the tools and skills to analyze data and use it to inform instructional decisions. 2. How does your school’s leadership team ensure ELLs have access to core instruction/curriculum? The school's instructional leadership team considers MLs/ELLs in all of our schools instructional design from kindergarten to 5th grade. To ensure the development and maintenance of an effective school environment for ELLs, the leadership team reviews the quality of the learning environment for the school as a whole. The goal is to ensure that teaching and learning activities are well–structured and culturally and linguistically engaging while allowing all students to be active participants within the lesson as well as acquisition of the English language and academic content. The following curricula is used at P.S. 14: HMH Into Reading, HMH Arriba a Lectura, Envision, Amplify Science, and the Department of Education Civics for all Curriculum. This extends to theater, art, STEAM engineering, Science, dance, music, and SEL. This also covers the 4 domains of learning a language -- speaking, listening, reading, and writing. Teacher's lessons and presentations are honored in home languages across the grades. Teachers are trained in CRSE and Leader in me. 3. Describe how core content is delivered in each program model. Specify language, and the instructional approaches and methods to foster language development and meet the demands of the Next Generation Learning Standards. Core Content Delivery Across Program Models At P.S. 014, core content is delivered through intentional integration of the four language modalities—speaking, listening, reading, and writing—across all subject areas. Instructional planning ensures that English Language Learners (ELLs) can access grade-level content while simultaneously building language proficiency. Teachers DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 166 of 292 embed scaffolds such as sentence starters, graphic organizers, bilingual glossaries, and visuals to support comprehension. Vocabulary instruction is purposefully integrated into every lesson and reinforced through content- specific language routines. Approaches like the Micro-lab Protocol or The Three Whys provide structured opportunities for students to deepen discussion skills and engage collaboratively with peers, positioning English- proficient classmates as natural language models. Project-based learning further contextualizes academic language by requiring students to apply knowledge to real-world, student-centered tasks. This design is aligned with the Next Generation Learning Standards (NGLS), which emphasize both content mastery and literacy practices. For example, when addressing the ELA standard RI.4.3 (Explain events, procedures, ideas, or concepts in a historical text), teachers scaffold by previewing key vocabulary in both English and Spanish, using visuals and guided questioning. Similarly, in Math (NGLS 4.OA.3: Solve multistep word problems), teachers employ sentence frames and bilingual word banks so that language does not obstruct problem-solving. Dual Language (Spanish-English Program) In the Dual Language program, core content is delivered in both English and Spanish on an alternating-day model. Instructional strategies include translanguaging practices, where students use their full linguistic repertoire to process and express understanding, and cognate recognition activities, which help students make cross-linguistic connections between Spanish and English academic vocabulary. Teachers make content comprehensible by layering supports: anchor charts in both languages, leveled texts from Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura, and bilingual math vocabulary walls aligned to Envisions. Lessons are planned with clear language and content objectives that are shared with students. For instance, in science (aligned to NGLS 4- PS3-2: Energy transfer), students may investigate heat transfer through a hands-on experiment while discussing observations in Spanish one day and writing their findings in English the next. This approach ensures biliteracy development while maintaining rigorous grade-level expectations. Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE – Spanish) In the Transitional Bilingual Education program, core content is delivered in both English and Spanish, except for ELA, which is taught exclusively in English. Instruction begins with a 60% Spanish / 40% English ratio and gradually shifts to 40% Spanish / 60% English as students progress. This gradual release model supports English acquisition while preserving home-language literacy as a foundation for transfer. Teachers use explicitly stated language and content objectives, aligned to the NGLS, to provide clarity and direction. For example, in social studies (aligned to NGLS 4.6: Civic participation and engagement), teachers may introduce a concept such as voting rights in Spanish, ensure comprehension through visuals and collaborative discussion, and then transition into English for reinforcement and extended writing. Scaffolds such as short video clips in both languages, sentence frames, and guided practice help ensure content mastery while developing proficiency in English. 4. What instructional strategies and grade-level materials, including technology, do teachers of MLs/ELLs use to provide access to grade level academic content areas and accelerate English language development? Include core content area and language materials with consideration to specific ELL subgroups. a. ELLs with IEPs Instructional Supports for ELLs with IEPs At P.S. 014, instructional supports are intentionally designed to address the intersection of language development DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 167 of 292 and disability-related needs for ELLs with IEPs. Strategies focus on making content accessible while fostering both English proficiency and individualized learning goals. Building on Background Knowledge Supports such as videos, images, and pre-reading in the home language provide multimodal entry points for students who may struggle with decoding or comprehension due to a disability. For example, pre-reading with audio support in the home language allows students to access content without being limited by reading difficulties. See-Think-Wonder routines are scaffolded to help students move from concrete visuals to abstract reasoning, an approach that benefits ELLs with disabilities by explicitly modeling the thought process and encouraging structured participation in discussions. Vocabulary Development Teachers pre-select high-utility words and pair them with visual cues, bold print, or highlighted text so students with attention or processing challenges can focus on the most essential terms. Cognates are explicitly taught to bridge home and English languages, supporting students with working memory difficulties by making meaningful linguistic connections. Picture dictionaries, glossaries, and word banks are used as accessible reference tools, giving students with IEP accommodations the ability to revisit language supports independently during tasks. Supporting Grade-Level Content Graphic organizers are tailored to disability needs (e.g., color-coded charts for students with executive functioning challenges). Chunking text and using sentence stems for turn-and-talk ensure that students who struggle with expressive language or organization can still actively participate. Previewing texts in the home language provides a foundation for students with reading disabilities, ensuring they enter lessons with accessible background knowledge. Collaborative Learning Structures Groups are formed intentionally using both language proficiency data and IEP goals (e.g., pairing a student working on expressive language skills with peers who can model sentence frames). Structured routines (like reciprocal teaching or role-based group work) provide predictability, which benefits students with autism spectrum disorders, ADHD, or other disabilities requiring clear expectations. Leveraging the Home Language as a Resource Teachers strategically use the home language for clarification, directions, or concept reinforcement, ensuring that disability-related challenges in English decoding or processing do not prevent access to rigorous content. Building Student Agency Lessons are designed around student choice and interest, motivating ELLs with IEPs who may require additional engagement strategies. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 168 of 292 Opportunities for small-group collaboration allow students with varying proficiency and ability levels to contribute at their own pace, while still being exposed to grade-level language models. Teachers explicitly elicit and validate prior knowledge, affirming student strengths and creating a sense of ownership that is especially important for students who may struggle academically. b. SIFE Teachers of Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners (MLs/ELLs) at P.S. 014 use a range of instructional strategies, grade-level materials, and technology tools to ensure that all students can access academic content while accelerating English language development. Special consideration is given to Students with Interrupted/Inconsistent Formal Education (SIFE), whose unique needs require carefully designed, targeted supports. Core Content and Language Materials In ELA, SIFE students work with Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura, supplemented by leveled texts, bilingual glossaries, and visual storyboards to build early literacy and comprehension. Instruction often emphasizes phonemic awareness, decoding, and oral storytelling to strengthen foundational skills before moving into complex texts. In Mathematics, teachers use Envisions alongside hands-on manipulatives, visuals, and bilingual math word banks to make content comprehensible. SIFE students benefit from scaffolded lessons that explicitly teach math vocabulary and problem-solving language. In Science and Social Studies, programs such as Amplify and Passport are adapted with graphic organizers, sentence frames, and pre-teaching of key vocabulary to ensure that language is not a barrier to content mastery. Instructional Strategies for SIFE Small-group instruction is central for SIFE students, focusing on early reading readiness, decoding, and oral fluency. Teachers integrate explicit routines and rituals into lessons to help students build the academic habits and classroom behaviors they may not have experienced due to interrupted schooling. Translanguaging and home-language supports are used intentionally, allowing students to draw on their first language as a bridge to English. For example, teachers preview texts in Spanish (or other home languages) before moving into English instruction. See-Think-Wonder routines, sentence stems, and collaborative discussion structures support the transition from concrete visuals to abstract academic reasoning. These structures help SIFE students practice oral English in meaningful, low-stakes ways. Graphic organizers and chunked texts are provided to scaffold comprehension, with frequent teacher check-ins to monitor understanding and address gaps in background knowledge. Technology Integration for SIFE SIFE students use Imagine Learning, Lexia, and Raz-Plus for literacy acceleration, with adaptive pathways that target decoding, vocabulary, and comprehension. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 169 of 292 Bilingual e-dictionaries, text-to-speech tools, and translation applications provide immediate language support, allowing students to engage with grade-level tasks while building independence. Parents of SIFE students are also given access to remote technology platforms, ensuring that families can support literacy development and language learning at home. Wraparound Supports for SIFE SIFE students are regularly reviewed in Student Intervention Team (SIT) meetings, where teachers collaborate to determine whether additional assessments, resources, or social-emotional supports are required. Instruction is often trauma-informed, recognizing that interrupted schooling may be tied to migration, resettlement, or displacement challenges. Teachers create supportive classroom environments that prioritize trust, routine, and consistency. Where possible, biliteracy approaches are integrated so that SIFE students can build on strengths in their home language while acquiring English. c. Newcomers At P.S. 014, Newcomers are defined as English Language Learners who have been receiving ENL services for 0–3 years. This group is not limited to Entering/Emerging students; it also includes Transitioning learners who, despite progressing in proficiency, are still developing the academic English needed to fully access grade-level content. Teachers hold Newcomers to the same Next Generation Learning Standards (NGLS) in Mathematics, English Language Arts, Science, and Social Studies as their monolingual peers, but instruction is strategically scaffolded to provide equitable access. Instructional planning emphasizes the home language as an instructional tool, allowing students to leverage their full linguistic repertoire to build content understanding and transfer skills into English. Instructional Strategies Planning Beyond HMH Scaffolds: While programs like Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura, Into Math, Amplify, and Passport provide baseline scaffolds, teachers at P.S. 014 intentionally plan additional supports, recognizing that the embedded modifications are insufficient for Newcomers. For example, before introducing an Into Reading text, teachers pre-teach academic vocabulary with visuals, use bilingual glossaries, and provide sentence frames tailored to the unit’s essential questions. Explicit Instruction and Modeling: Teachers integrate direct modeling of both language and content. This includes teacher think-alouds, peer modeling, and structured turn-and-talks with sentence starters. Writing tasks are scaffolded with graphic organizers and rubrics simplified into step-by-step expectations that still align with grade- level standards. Vocabulary Development: Academic vocabulary is reinforced through visuals, cognate connections, word banks, and bilingual anchor charts. Teachers frequently build connections between English and Spanish, highlighting linguistic similarities to deepen comprehension. Collaborative Learning: Students participate in cooperative structures such as jigsaw reading, reciprocal teaching, and peer-led discussions, where English-proficient peers serve as language models. These structures allow Newcomers to engage in authentic academic discourse while building confidence. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 170 of 292 Multimodal Supports: Technology tools like Nearpod, Flipgrid, and Peardeck are integrated with immersive readers and translation features, giving Newcomers access to both oral and written supports across content areas. Teachers also embed short videos, images, and home-language texts to build background knowledge before engaging in complex lessons. Core Materials by Content Area ELA: Into Reading/Arriba La Lectura supplemented with leveled readers, bilingual glossaries, and teacher-created sentence frames to scaffold text-based responses. Math: Into Math/Envisions supported with manipulatives, bilingual word walls, and chunked problem-solving routines that explicitly address math vocabulary. Science: Amplify paired with pre-lab vocabulary previews, graphic organizers for observations, and bilingual sentence starters for claims, evidence, and reasoning. Social Studies: Passport supplemented with primary source visuals, bilingual timelines, and structured debates/discussions using sentence stems. Progress Monitoring and Data Use To track growth, teachers use Exact Path, mCLASS, and MAP Growth in Math. Results are analyzed collaboratively to identify students requiring intervention. Supports are then delivered in English, Spanish, or the student’s home language, depending on the area of need, ensuring both language development and content mastery. d. Long Term ELLs As per the Policy and Reference Guide for SY 24-25, Long-term ELLs (completed 6+ years) are given an additional one-on-one intervention in the modality that is needed. Since an elementary school cannot have Long Term ELLs (LITELs) by definition, the goal is to adjust learning so that there are no longer students who are not receiving the support necessary to overcome language barriers and need to learn. PS. 14 has included RTI support in specifically targeted classrooms where an additional ESOL provider supports small-group instruction. To understand the benefits of this additional resource, ESOL teachers monitor student growth; we are using Exact Path, mCLASS, and MAPS Math to monitor student growth. We then will use this data to support through various intervention systems in English, Spanish, or the student's Home language. The implementation of an ENL RTI system that uses real-world data, co-planning, and co-teaching, where small group instruction is underway, allows for strategic placement of learning and activities where students can productively struggle and acquire the necessary language skills to grow in the all content areas. 5. What supports does your school provide to ELLs with IEPs to achieve their IEP goals and attain English language proficiency within the least restrictive environment? At P.S. 014, ENL teachers are fully aware of students’ IEP goals and integrate them into daily lesson planning and instruction. Prior to service delivery, ENL providers review each student’s IEP to identify specific academic, social- emotional, and language-based objectives. This ensures that English language development activities are not isolated, but directly support students’ progress toward their individualized learning targets. Collaborative Co-Planning ENL teachers co-plan regularly with special education teachers to align lesson objectives, scaffolds, and DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 171 of 292 assessments. During co-planning, teams discuss: How to embed IEP goals (e.g., expressive/receptive language, decoding, written expression) into ENL lessons, Which instructional strategies (e.g., guided reading, writing frames, speech-to-text technology) best support both language acquisition and IEP progress, How to adapt grade-level HMH core materials (Into Reading, Into Math, Amplify, Passport) so that they remain rigorous yet accessible. This collaboration ensures that students receive consistent supports across settings and that ENL instruction meaningfully contributes to their overall IEP progress. Instructional Strategies in ENL Sessions ENL instruction for IEP students is delivered both in integrated and stand-alone settings within homeroom classrooms so students do not miss core instruction. Supports are tailored to IEP goals and language needs, including: Picture labeling and visual supports to aid comprehension and recall, Sentence stems and structured dialogues to strengthen expressive language, Graphic organizers and color-coded texts to build comprehension and support executive functioning skills, Chunking texts and guided reading to address decoding and fluency goals, Video clips and visuals to build background knowledge and strengthen vocabulary acquisition, Small-group and guided instruction to allow for targeted intervention aligned with both ENL and IEP objectives. Collaboration in Practice For example, if a student’s IEP goal is to improve written expression, the ENL and special education teachers co- design writing tasks where the ENL teacher scaffolds academic vocabulary and sentence structure while the special education teacher addresses mechanics and organization. If a student is working on oral communication, ENL providers embed structured turn-and-talk routines with sentence starters, while the special education teacher monitors progress toward expressive language benchmarks. 6. How is home language assessed in each program model (DLBE, TBE, and ENL)? In the Dual Language (DL) program, the home language (Spanish) is assessed systematically to ensure balanced literacy development across both Spanish and English. Teachers administer the following assessments in Spanish: Arriba La Lectura Benchmark Assessments are used to measure students’ reading levels, fluency, and comprehension in Spanish. Results guide teachers in grouping students for small-group instruction, planning guided reading lessons, and selecting leveled texts for independent reading. mCLASS by Amplify (Spanish literacy measures) are used for foundational skill checks, such as phonemic DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 172 of 292 awareness, phonics, and oral reading fluency. Data from mCLASS help teachers identify decoding gaps and plan phonics mini-lessons in Spanish that transfer into English literacy. Exact Path by Edmentum provides adaptive diagnostics in Spanish. Teachers use growth reports to align targeted practice with students’ areas of need in vocabulary and comprehension. Aprendo Leyendo is administered to track Spanish reading fluency and comprehension progress. Data are used to reinforce biliteracy goals, ensuring that students build parallel skills in both languages. Instructional planning leverages these assessments to balance Spanish Language Arts (SLA) with English Language Arts (ELA). For example, a student struggling with fluency in Spanish but strong in decoding in English may receive targeted fluency work in Spanish, while transferring comprehension strategies into English through translanguaging routines. Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) Program In the TBE program, Spanish home-language assessment ensures that students maintain literacy in their first language while gradually transitioning to English. Teachers administer: Arriba La Lectura Benchmark Assessments to monitor growth in Spanish reading, writing, and phonics. Results inform small-group NLA instruction and help teachers scaffold ELA lessons using students’ Spanish literacy strengths. mCLASS by Amplify (Spanish measures) to assess phonological awareness, phonics, and early fluency. Teachers use these results to plan foundational skills instruction in Spanish that supports early literacy while bridging to English. Exact Path by Edmentum to provide adaptive, individualized support in Spanish literacy. Reports allow teachers to target skill gaps in vocabulary and comprehension, then design interventions that align with NLA and ELA standards. Assessment data in TBE are explicitly tied to planning for the Spanish-to-English transition. For instance, if a student demonstrates strong comprehension in Spanish but struggles in English, teachers plan parallel lessons that first reinforce concepts in Spanish and then use English-language scaffolds (e.g., cognates, bilingual glossaries) to transfer the skill. 7. Describe systems and structures for supporting co-teaching and collaboration among teachers for all ELL program models available at your school (e.g. integrated co-teaching, Dual Language Bilingual Education teacher partnerships, etc.). At P.S. 014, co-teaching and collaboration among ENL and content teachers are essential to providing comprehensive, equitable support for English Language Learners (ELLs). Structures are in place to ensure that instruction is intentional, aligned to program models, and reflective of students’ language and academic needs. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 173 of 292 1. Establishing Shared Vision and Goals Teachers working with ELLs share a unified understanding of program expectations, student needs, and the role of collaboration in advancing both academic achievement and English language development. Norms for co-teaching are developed collaboratively so that roles, responsibilities, and communication channels are clearly defined. 2. Professional Development and Training Ongoing professional learning supports teachers in strengthening co-teaching practices. Topics include integrated instructional strategies, scaffolding aligned to Next Generation Learning Standards (NGLS), and culturally responsive teaching. Teachers also engage in peer observations and feedback cycles to refine collaborative practices. 3. Instructional Delivery Models Integrated Instruction: ENL and content teachers co-plan and co-teach within the classroom. Instruction is jointly delivered through approaches such as parallel teaching, station teaching, or one teach/one support, depending on lesson objectives. Both teachers share responsibility for instruction and assessment, ensuring that content and language objectives are met. Stand-Alone Instruction: ENL teachers provide direct language development instruction within the classroom setting, often working with small groups. These sessions focus on targeted language skills, literacy development, and academic vocabulary that directly support grade-level content learning. Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE): In DLBE classrooms, the bilingual teacher and English-speaking partner teacher collaborate to ensure that instruction is balanced across both languages. Planning is intentionally aligned so that concepts taught in Spanish are reinforced in English and vice versa. 4. Collaborative Planning Time Dedicated time for ENL and content teachers to co-plan is built into the school schedule. Grade teams and ENL staff meet during weekly common planning periods to: Align language objectives with content objectives, Analyze student performance data and IEP goals where applicable, Co-develop scaffolds and instructional strategies that ensure access to grade-level material, Adjust pacing and groupings based on ongoing progress monitoring. This time is protected and prioritized, ensuring that collaborative planning is consistent and meaningful rather than ad hoc. 5. Leveraging Technology and Resources Co-teachers use shared digital planning platforms (Google Docs, Drive, and Classroom) to co-create lessons and share resources. Language-support tools such as Immersive Reader, bilingual glossaries, and Exact Path are integrated into planning to accelerate language development and reinforce core instruction. 6. Inclusive School Culture The school fosters a culture that values collaboration, cultural diversity, and shared accountability. Successful partnerships are highlighted, and teachers are encouraged to reflect on practice, celebrate student successes, and continually refine their co-teaching approaches. Describe your school’s professional development plan in order to meet the mandated Continuing Teacher and Leader Education (CTLE) ELL specific hours for certification renewal as per CR Part 154 DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 176 of 292 (15% of total hours for all certified faculty [holders of professional certificates in the classroom teaching service, educational leadership service and level III teaching assistant certificate holders] and 50% of total hours for bilingual education/ENL teachers receive ELL-specific professional development). Meeting the mandated Continuing Teacher and Leader Education (CTLE) ELL-specific hours for certification renewal, as outlined in CR Part 154, is essential for ensuring that all certified faculty are fully prepared to meet the academic and social-emotional needs of English Language Learners (ELLs) at P.S. 014. Annual CTLE Allocation Ensure that 15% of all CTLE hours are allocated for ELL-specific training for general education teachers. Which screening assessments (e.g.iReady, MAP Growth, Acadience, or STAR Reading) does your school use to guide instructional planning for your ELLs? mCLASS by Amplify is an assessment and data analysis platform that can be a valuable tool for guiding instructional planning for English Language Learners (ELLs) and addressing data through targeted interventions. Here's how you can use mCLASS for this purpose: **Using mCLASS to Guide Instructional Planning for ELLs:** 1. **Initial Assessment:** - Administer mCLASS assessments to ELLs to assess their language and literacy skills. These assessments may cover areas like phonemic awareness, phonics, fluency, vocabulary, and comprehension. 2. **Data Collection and Analysis:** - Collect and analyze the assessment data to identify each ELL's specific strengths and weaknesses in language and literacy. - Use the data to determine the proficiency levels of ELLs in various skill areas. 3. **Proficiency Level Identification:** - mCLASS often categorizes students into proficiency levels (e.g., below benchmark, approaching benchmark, on benchmark, above benchmark). Use this information to understand where each ELL stands in relation to grade-level benchmarks. 4. **Individualized Learning Goals:** - Based on the assessment data and proficiency level, set individualized learning goals for each ELL. These goals should address the specific areas where they need improvement. 5. **Tailored Instructional Strategies:** - Select instructional strategies and materials that are aligned with the identified needs of ELLs. - Adapt curriculum materials and lessons to provide targeted support in areas of weakness. **Addressing Data through Interventions for ELLs:** 1. **Progress Monitoring:** - Regularly assess ELLs using mCLASS to monitor their progress toward their individualized goals. - Track their movement across proficiency levels to evaluate the effectiveness of interventions. 2. **Data-Driven Decision-Making:** - Use mCLASS data to make informed decisions about the need for interventions. If ELLs are not making expected progress, it may be time to implement interventions. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 179 of 292 3. **Intervention Planning:** - Identify specific interventions or strategies that are appropriate for addressing the needs of ELLs. Interventions may include: - Small-group instruction focused on targeted skills. - One-on-one tutoring or support. - Use of research-based intervention programs. - Additional time for language and literacy development. 4. **Differentiation:** - Differentiate instruction within the regular classroom based on ELLs' needs and mCLASS data. - Provide varying levels of support, scaffolding, and resources to meet individual needs. 5. **Regular Progress Monitoring:** - Continuously assess ELLs to track their response to interventions. - Adjust and modify interventions as needed based on ongoing data analysis. 6. **Communication and Reporting:** - Communicate progress and intervention outcomes to ELLs, their parents or guardians, and other relevant stakeholders. - Share data reports generated by mCLASS to demonstrate growth and inform decision-making. 7. **Professional Development:** - Utilize mCLASS data to identify areas where educators may need additional training or professional development to better support ELLs. - Offer targeted professional development opportunities to enhance teachers' capacity to address ELLs' needs effectively. By consistently using mCLASS to assess, monitor, and analyze data, educators can make data-driven decisions to guide instructional planning and provide timely and appropriate interventions to support the language and literacy development of ELLs. This approach helps ensure that ELLs receive the individualized support they need to succeed academically. 12. For all grades, describe how your school uses data to guide instruction for ELLs within a Multi- Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). Refer to the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs and ELLs and MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs to help in the development and implementation of your school’s plan for MLs/ELLs. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 180 of 292 At P.S. 14 we use data to guide instruction for ELLs within a Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) framework in a systematic and comprehensive way, as outlined in the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs and ELLs and MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs. This approach involves using data to inform decisions at all tiers of instruction and intervention, ensuring that ELLs receive the appropriate support to meet their individual needs. Tier 1: Universal Instruction Universal Screening: Administer universal screening assessments in both English and the student's home language, if available, to identify ELLs who may be at risk for academic difficulties. Formative Assessment: Use ongoing formative assessments to monitor ELLs' progress and adjust instruction as needed. This includes observations, classroom activities, and quizzes. Data Analysis: Analyze data from universal screening and formative assessments to identify areas of strength and need for ELLs as a whole and for individual students. Differentiated Instruction: Use data to differentiate instruction for ELLs, providing scaffolding, language supports, and various learning modalities to ensure access to the curriculum. Tier 2: Targeted Interventions Progress Monitoring: Regularly monitor ELLs' progress in Tier 2 interventions using curriculum-based measures (CBMs) or other appropriate assessments. Data-Based Decision Making: Use progress monitoring data to determine the effectiveness of interventions and make adjustments as needed. Intensive Language Support: Provide intensive language support to ELLs who are not making sufficient progress in Tier 2 interventions, focusing on specific language skills and academic vocabulary. Tier 3: Intensive Interventions Individualized Support: Develop individualized learning plans for ELLs who require Tier 3 interventions, based on their specific needs and strengths. Frequent Progress Monitoring: Monitor ELLs' progress in Tier 3 interventions more frequently (e.g., weekly) to ensure that they are responding to the intensified support. Data-Based Individualization: Use progress monitoring data to individualize instruction and interventions further, adjusting the frequency, duration, and intensity of support as needed. Additional Considerations: Cultural and Linguistic Responsiveness: Ensure that data collection and analysis consider ELLs' cultural and linguistic backgrounds. Collaboration: Foster collaboration among teachers, administrators, and support staff to share data, discuss student progress, and make informed decisions about instruction and intervention. Professional Development: Provide ongoing professional development for teachers on data analysis, differentiated instruction, and culturally responsive teaching practices. By using data to guide instruction for ELLs within an MTSS framework, schools can ensure that all students receive the support they need to succeed academically and linguistically. FORMATIVE DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 181 of 292 14. Which formative assessments (e.g. NYC performance Tasks, ELL Periodic Assessment, HLA Assessment, DRA, teacher-created assessments, etc.) are used to inform and drive instruction? At P.S. 014, teachers implement a comprehensive formative assessment system aligned with the current HMH Into Reading curriculum to monitor student progress and guide instructional decisions for English Language Learners (ELLs). Teachers use a variety of assessment tools that move beyond traditional running records and provide a clearer picture of students’ growth in both content knowledge and language development. HMH Into Reading Assessments include: Pre-module assessments that provide baseline data on students’ comprehension, vocabulary, and background knowledge prior to each new unit. Weekly reading assessments based on the literature and informational texts taught during the week, ensuring alignment to grade-level standards and the Next Generation Learning Standards (NGLS). End-of-book assessments that measure cumulative understanding of literary elements, comprehension strategies, and text-based analysis. Writing assessments that focus on vocabulary use, sentence structure, grammar, and application of writing conventions. End-of-module writing assessments that require extended responses and evidence-based writing aligned to module themes. Teacher-Created and Differentiated Assessments: Teachers adapt assessments to reflect students’ English proficiency levels. For example, sentence frames, bilingual glossaries, or visuals may be embedded into reading comprehension tasks. Writing assessments may be scaffolded to allow students to demonstrate mastery of content while progressing in English proficiency. Running Records: While no longer the primary measure of reading progress, teachers may still administer running records as a supplementary tool for specific students, particularly newcomers or students with IEPs, to provide individualized insights into fluency, decoding, and accuracy. These assessment structures ensure that teachers have ongoing, actionable data to inform instructional planning, identify areas for targeted intervention, and monitor English language development while maintaining access to grade-level content. 15. How do you ensure that MLs/ELLs are appropriately evaluated in their home languages throughout the year? Bilingual teachers assess their students in their home language of Spanish at the beginning, middle, and end of the school year each year. They also administer unit math tests in Spanish. All teachers use rubrics and performance criteria to asses their student's work. These rubrics are based on language proficiency and academic progression through student work such as presentations, written assignments, and or reading activities. Students also perform oral presentations, written assessments, and portfolios, Teachers assess their students through role-plays, interviews, oral reports, and summarizing/paraphrasing a piece of text. Students also set their own learning goals, and reassess them as they reach them. Furthermore, with the addition of mCLASS by Amplify, students who are in TBE and DL language programs will be assessed in their home language (Spanish) as well as in English at least three DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 182 of 292 times for periodic purposes, then progress will be monitored over the course of the school year before, during, and at the end of each unit of study. Which summative assessments (e.g. NYSESLAT, ELA, Math, Regents, etc.) are used to evaluate the effectiveness of your ML/ELL programs and how does it inform instructional design and curricular decisions? (Refer to the ELL Data Analysis Tool). We test all our ELLs with the NYSESLAT in the Spring. All students 3-5th grade are administered the State ELA and Math tests each year unless the student is a newcomer; then, they are exempt for their first year on the ELA. When this Language Policy Plan is completed, NYS Grade's 3-8 tests have not been turned over for evaluation/review of scores. When scores are provided, administrators and teachers are all given areas to discuss creating appropriate instructional design and curriculum decisions. To do this, grade teams, admin teams, and professional learning communities do a deep dive and do an analysis on summative assessments that plays a crucial role in informing instructional design and curricular decisions for English Language Learners (ELLs). Summative assessments are typically administered at the end of a learning period or course to evaluate students' learning. Here's how the analysis of these assessments can inform instructional planning and curriculum choices for ELLs: **1. Identifying Learning Gaps:** - Analyzing summative assessment data allows educators to identify specific areas where ELLs may have knowledge or skill gaps. - It helps pinpoint which concepts or skills were not mastered, indicating where additional instruction is needed. **2. Understanding Proficiency Levels:** - Summative assessments can provide insights into ELLs' proficiency levels in various content areas. - This understanding helps educators tailor instruction to meet the language and academic needs of ELLs at their specific proficiency levels. **3. Assessing Language Proficiency:** - Summative assessments can reveal ELLs' language proficiency levels, including their ability to comprehend and produce academic language. - This information informs decisions about language development support and language-rich instruction. **4. Identifying Common Misconceptions:** - Summative assessment analysis can uncover common misconceptions or challenges that ELLs face in understanding content. - This knowledge guides teachers in addressing these misconceptions through targeted instruction. **5. Informing Curriculum Adaptations:** - Based on the assessment data, educators can adapt or modify the curriculum to better suit the needs of ELLs. - Curriculum adaptations may involve incorporating additional scaffolding, visuals, real-world connections, or simplified language. **6. Differentiation Strategies:** - Summative assessment results help in the differentiation of instruction. - Teachers can identify ELLs who need more support and design interventions or enrichment activities accordingly. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 183 of 292 **7. Aligning Instructional Resources:** - Assessment data can inform decisions about the selection of instructional materials and resources. - Teachers can choose resources that align with the specific content and language needs of their ELLs. **8. Targeted Professional Development:** - For educators working with ELLs, the analysis of summative assessments can highlight areas where they may need professional development. - Training can focus on strategies for teaching ELLs in specific content areas or addressing common challenges. **9. Monitoring Progress Over Time:** - Summative assessment data can be used to track ELLs' progress over time, helping educators evaluate the effectiveness of instructional strategies and interventions. **10. Adjusting Instructional Strategies:** - Teachers can use assessment data to adjust their instructional strategies, such as modifying teaching methods, providing additional practice opportunities, or incorporating formative assessments to gauge ongoing progress. **11. Individualized Learning Plans:** - Assessment data may lead to developing individualized learning plans for ELLs, outlining specific goals and strategies to address their unique needs. **12. Data-Driven Decision-Making:** - Overall, the analysis of summative assessments promotes data-driven decision-making, ensuring that instructional choices are based on evidence of student learning. In summary, the analysis of summative assessments for ELLs is a valuable tool for tailoring instruction, curricular decisions, and support strategies to meet their unique language and academic needs. It empowers educators to make informed decisions to improve ELLs' learning outcomes and foster their academic success. 17. What is your NYSESLAT administration plan? How does your school ensure that current and former ELLs receive necessary accommodations for state assessments, including the Specialized High School Admissions Test (SHSAT) if applicable? At P.S. 014 Fairview, ensuring that current and former English Language Learners (ELLs) receive all required accommodations on state assessments is a structured, collaborative process grounded in CR Part 154, NYSED testing guidelines, and the school’s internal accountability systems. These accommodations are implemented consistently to ensure equitable access to grade-level assessments while accurately measuring student achievement. 1. Identification and Documentation of Accommodations All current ELLs are reviewed prior to each assessment window to ensure that accommodations mandated under CR Part 154 are in place. This includes extended time, separate locations, bilingual glossaries, translated test directions, and use of the home language where permitted. For former ELLs in the mandated two-year monitoring period, testing accommodations continue as required. Student records are carefully cross-checked in ATS and NYSESLAT histories to ensure eligibility. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 185 of 292 For ELLs with IEPs, ENL and special education teachers co-plan to ensure that language-related accommodations and IEP-mandated testing accommodations (e.g., scribing, text-to-speech, reduced-distraction settings) are fully aligned. 2. Integration of Home Language Supports In Dual Language (DL) and Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) classrooms, teachers leverage home-language assessments and resources to prepare students for bilingual testing options. Assessments such as Arriba La Lectura benchmarks, mCLASS (Spanish measures), Exact Path, and Aprendo Leyendo provide data to identify whether students should test in Spanish or English, and to guide targeted supports in both languages. Teachers intentionally plan for cognate instruction, translanguaging, and bilingual glossaries, so students are familiar with accommodations they will encounter on state assessments. 3. Instructional Preparation and Formative Assessment Formative assessments embedded in the HMH Into Reading curriculum (pre-module assessments, weekly reading checks, end-of-book and end-of-module writing assessments) prepare ELLs for the structure and rigor of state exams. Assessments are differentiated using visuals, sentence stems, and home-language resources so students are consistently exposed to scaffolded versions of grade-level tasks. Math (Into Math/Envisions), Science (Amplify), and Social Studies (Passport) modules are planned with language objectives aligned to content objectives, ensuring that language supports are embedded in the instruction leading up to state assessments. 4. Collaborative Planning and Professional Development Weekly common planning periods are built into the master schedule for ENL, content, and special education teachers to co-plan. During these meetings, teachers align accommodations with lesson delivery, ensuring consistency between classroom practice and state testing conditions. Professional development for staff—including administrators, teachers, and paraprofessionals—emphasizes assessment accommodations for MLs/ELLs, and is delivered by state-accredited CTLE providers. Sessions cover how to integrate language scaffolds into daily instruction and how to support students with testing accommodations such as oral translations, extended time, or bilingual glossaries. 5. Monitoring and Compliance A testing accommodations checklist is maintained by the ENL compliance team to ensure every current and former ELL is correctly coded and receives their entitled supports. The Student Intervention Team (SIT) and grade-level PLCs review progress-monitoring data (Exact Path, mCLASS, MAP Growth) to identify at-risk ELLs and former ELLs and ensure that accommodations are not only in place but also aligned to students’ demonstrated needs. Accommodations implementation is monitored during testing by the testing coordinator, ENL supervisor, and proctors trained in administering assessments to multilingual learners. 6. Subgroup Considerations (IEPs, SIFE, Newcomers, Former ELLs) ELLs with IEPs: ENL teachers are fully aware of IEP goals and co-plan with special education teachers to ensure that testing accommodations reflect both language needs and disability-related supports. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 186 of 292 SIFE students: Prior to assessments, SIFE students receive additional small-group instruction targeting foundational literacy, vocabulary development, and academic routines, supported by technology (Imagine Learning, Lexia, Raz- Plus). Accommodations such as extended time and separate locations are emphasized to reduce stress and allow processing time. Newcomers (0–3 years of service): Teachers scaffold assessments with visuals, bilingual glossaries, and simplified rubrics throughout the year so that testing accommodations mirror instructional supports. Former ELLs: All former ELLs in their two-year monitoring period continue to receive full testing accommodations as required, ensuring continuity of access during this critical transition phase. Annual Individual Meeting to discuss goals of the program, language development progress, language proficiency assessment results, and language development needs in all content areas. Purpose of the Annual Individual Meeting The required annual individual meeting is designed to provide parents/guardians of English Language Learners (ELLs) with a comprehensive update on their child’s language development and academic progress. This meeting is distinct from the ELL program orientation and DOE parent-teacher conferences, and it serves as a dedicated opportunity to: Review the Goals of the ENL Program: Teachers explain how the school’s English as a New Language (ENL) program supports English language development while ensuring that students meet grade-level expectations in all content areas. Families learn how program goals—such as building biliteracy, promoting academic achievement, and developing socio-emotional skills—connect to their child’s individual learning plan. Discuss Language Development Progress: Teachers share updates on the student’s progress across all four domains—listening, speaking, reading, and writing DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 189 of 292 —using classroom evidence and formative assessments (e.g., HMH Into Reading assessments, writing samples, vocabulary checks). Parents are shown specific examples of student work to illustrate growth, and areas needing improvement are clearly identified. Explain English Language Proficiency Assessment Results: Families receive the student’s most recent NYSESLAT results or other proficiency data (e.g., mCLASS, Exact Path). Teachers explain the student’s proficiency level, describe what that level means in practical terms, and outline next steps for continued growth. Comparisons to previous results are used to show progress over time and to highlight whether the student is on track toward achieving Commanding status. Identify Language Development Needs in All Content Areas: Teachers and families collaboratively review the student’s performance in ELA, Math, Science, and Social Studies to identify language-based challenges, such as academic vocabulary, decoding word problems, or writing extended responses. Instructional strategies and supports—such as integrated ENL instruction, stand-alone ENL periods, scaffolds, and use of home language resources—are discussed to show how these needs will be addressed. Meeting Logistics Timing: Held annually, preferably in the first semester. Participants: Parent/guardian(s), ENL teacher, classroom/content teachers, and support staff (e.g., special education teacher if applicable). Translation/Interpretation: Professional interpreters are provided so families can fully participate in their preferred language. Documentation: Detailed notes of attendance, topics discussed, and agreed-upon actions are recorded and placed in the student’s cumulative folder, per NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs. Networking Opportunities: Dedicated spaces are created at leadership meetings and family workshops where ML/ELL families can connect with each other and build supportive networks. Clear and Accessible Information Simplified Explanations: Roles and responsibilities within PA, SLT, and CEC are explained in plain, jargon-free language so all families can easily understand expectations. Visual Aids: Infographics, flowcharts, and short videos are used to show how leadership bodies function and how decisions impact the school community. Open Q&A Sessions: Sessions are scheduled at flexible times to allow families to ask questions, seek clarification, and practice using leadership terminology in both English and their home languages. Mentorship for ML/ELL Parents: Experienced parent leaders mentor ML/ELL families who are new to leadership roles, providing one-on-one guidance and support in their preferred language. Ongoing Support and Recognition Training and Resources: We provide ongoing training in leadership skills, communication strategies, and DOE policy, delivered in multiple languages to ensure full accessibility. Recognition of ML/ELL Leaders: ML/ELL parents serving in leadership roles are celebrated through newsletters, award recognitions, and cultural events to acknowledge their contributions and encourage continued involvement. 3. Describe the structures and process in place for identification of ELLs year-round as required by the NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, including your school’s identification plan for any incoming students with an Individualized Education Plan (IEP). Include: How your school proactively plans for the ELL identification process so that it does not interrupt the provision of mandated ENL and/or bilingual instruction to current, former and potential ELLs, Who is responsible for each step of the ELL identification process, and School’s plan for ensuring ELLs and former ELLs will continue to receive mandated instruction. 1. Proactive Planning and Continuity of Services To prevent disruption to services, the ENL Compliance Lead and main office staff monitor all new registrations on a rolling basis, including kindergarten entry and mid-year transfers. Identification procedures are embedded into registration so that ENL and bilingual services begin immediately after eligibility is confirmed. ENL and bilingual classes are programmed into the master schedule before academic intervention services (AIS) or DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 196 of 292 other supports, ensuring compliance with mandated service minutes. Former ELLs are scheduled for monitoring services for two years post-exit, as required by the PRG, ensuring uninterrupted access to supports. 2. Mandated Identification Steps (PRG, p. 23) The school follows the step-by-step process required under CR Part 154 and the PRG: Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS): Completed by every newly enrolled student’s family during registration. Administered with the support of a qualified interpreter in the family’s preferred language to ensure accuracy. Reviewed immediately by the main office and ENL Compliance Lead to flag potential ELLs. Family Interview: Conducted by trained, certified staff to verify HLIS responses. Families are asked follow-up questions to confirm whether the student has been educated in a language other than English. NYSITELL Administration: Students flagged by the HLIS and interview are administered the New York State Identification Test for English Language Learners (NYSITELL) within 10 school days of enrollment. The Assistant Principal oversees compliance; the ENL Compliance Lead coordinates test administration, scoring, and entry into ATS/ELPC. Parent Notification and Program Placement: Families of students who qualify as ELLs receive Parent Notification Letters in their home language, as mandated by the PRG. Families are invited to a bilingual orientation session where program options (Transitional Bilingual Education, Dual Language, or stand-alone ENL) are explained. Parents sign documentation acknowledging receipt of this information, which is filed in the student’s cumulative folder. 3. Identification of ELLs with IEPs When students enter with an IEP, the ENL Compliance Lead collaborates with the school’s IEP team to ensure identification procedures meet both language and disability-related requirements. Following PRG Section 3.4, the team reviews whether the student previously received bilingual special education services and, if necessary, arranges for evaluations in the home language. The school ensures that ELLs with IEPs are scheduled for bilingual or ENL services without conflict with mandated DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 197 of 292 special education supports. Interpreters or bilingual service providers participate in IEP meetings to support placement and planning. 4. Roles and Responsibilities Main Office Staff & Parent Coordinator: Administer HLIS, arrange interpreters, and collect initial documents. ENL Compliance Lead: Reviews HLIS, conducts family interviews, coordinates NYSITELL administration, and ensures ATS/ELPC updates. Assistant Principal: Supervises compliance, ensures timelines are met, and oversees program placement. IEP Team & Special Education Teachers: Collaborate with ENL staff to align IEP goals with language development needs. Classroom Teachers: Implement initial scaffolds and supports for newly identified ELLs while formal programming begins. 5. Safeguards for Current and Former ELLs Weekly audits of ATS, STARS, and RSET are conducted by the ENL Compliance Lead to confirm that all eligible students, including transfers, are properly coded and receiving services. Former ELLs continue to receive supports and monitoring for two years post-exit, as mandated, and are flagged in ATS for tracking. Program continuity is protected by scheduling ENL/bilingual services first and maintaining bilingual staff availability for immediate placement. 6. Family Engagement and Language Access The school uses its allocated Language Access Funds to provide translated HLIS forms, Parent Notification Letters, and interpreters for family interviews and orientation sessions. Outreach is conducted in families’ home languages to ensure parents understand the identification process and their child’s program options. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 198 of 292 Principal Certification In accordance with New York State’s Commissioner’s Regulations Part 154 as outlined and implemented in the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, I, attest that the following ELL identification procedures and activities are adhered to, and staff are trained on the items listed below. The ELL Identification Process is completed for each student within 10 school days of enrollment (20 school days for students entering with IEPs). Enrollment status of each newly admitted student is determined: a. If a student has been in New York State public schools within the past 2 years, our school contacts the previous school to obtain ELL status, relevant assessment scores, and English language proficiency level. b. If a student has been outside of New York State public schools for more than 2 years, our school administers the ELL Identification Process as part of reentry. The home language of the student is determined by a trained and licensed pedagogue. a. The parent completes the Home Language Identification Survey in the parent’s preferred language; translation services are provided by a bilingual staff member, the DOE’s over-the-phone translation services, or an alternative, high quality translation service. An interview with parents and students in English and the home language is conducted. This includes reviewing documents, prior assessments, and academic experience. An assessment of the student’s abilities and/or work samples determines the student’s literacy and math level in his/her home language. Eligibility for the NYSITELL is determined. a. If the newly enrolled student is entering with an IEP, the school’s Language Proficiency Team (LPT) recommends eligibility to the principal, who obtains final approval from the superintendent. Students are administered the NYSITELL, if eligible. Notification letters are sent to the parent/guardian in their preferred language. a. Parent is notified of their child’s ELL status and results of the NYSITELL. If the student is identified as an ELL and the student’s home language is Spanish, the student is administered the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (LAB). Student with Interrupted/Inconsistent Formal Education (SIFE) status is determined using New York State’s resources. If a student is a newly identified ELL, the parent is invited to the parent orientation meeting. a. The parent orientation meeting provides parents/guardians with an explanation of each ELL program model offered in NYC; the parent video describing the three programs is presented (in parent’s preferred language); parents/guardians are encouraged to ask questions so that they are DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 199 of 292 informed about all ELL programs; translation and interpretation services are provided throughout the meeting. ELL is placed in the ELL program that the parent selected. a. If the bilingual program that the parent selected is not available, the parent is offered a transfer to a school with that bilingual program in accordance with NYC DOE transfer policies. b. If the ELL remains in the school, the ELL is placed in English as a New Language and our school keeps track of parent selection so that when minimum thresholds are met, we can open the ELL program of choice. Parents are notified that they have 45 days to appeal the ELL status of their child (ELL Re-identification Process). If the ELL Re-identification Process is implemented for any student, the principal reviews the decision of any student whose ELL status was changed because of the ELL Re-identification Process within 6 to 12 months. Copies of documentation regarding ELL identification, including letters and parent notifications are kept in the students’ cumulative files. Describe the resources and processes your school will use to communicate with parents who speak a language other than English during an emergency (e.g., lockdown, fire, student attendance, nurse/hospital visit, etc.), or when a parent needs to contact the school in an emergency, ensuring communication is in their preferred language. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 207 of 292 At P.S. 014 Fairview, the school has developed a comprehensive and proactive communication protocol to ensure that all families—particularly those who speak a language other than English—receive timely, accurate, and linguistically appropriate information during emergencies. This approach is fully aligned with the NYC DOE’s **Language Access Policy** and the **2024–2025 Policy & Reference Guide for Multilingual Learners/ELLs**, which mandates equitable access to communication for all families regardless of their home language. In the event of a schoolwide emergency such as a lockdown, fire drill, evacuation, or other critical situation, the school activates a multi-layered communication plan. A team of designated bilingual staff, including the Parent Coordinator and main office personnel, are mobilized to assist with immediate translation and outreach. To support this work, the school maintains a library of pre-approved and pre-translated DOE emergency notification templates —most commonly in Spanish, given the school’s language demographics. These templates are used to send out messages through **School Messenger** and **Operoo**, both of which are configured to deliver communications in families’ preferred languages as indicated in ATS. For urgent situations that require real-time communication in less common languages, the school relies on the **DOE’s Translation and Interpretation Unit (T\&I Unit)** and **Language Line**, a telephonic interpretation service available on demand. In health-related emergencies, such as when a student is sent to the nurse or requires ambulance transport, the school ensures that the parent or guardian is contacted immediately in their preferred language. The school nurse has direct access to Language Line to communicate effectively with families in real time. In addition, the nurse and office staff have access to translated medical notification forms to support consistent and clear follow-up. When a parent contacts the school during an emergency, either by phone or in person, staff follow established procedures to ensure that the parent can communicate in their preferred language without delay. All emergency contact cards are reviewed at the start of the year to identify home languages, and this information is used to route calls to bilingual staff or to initiate interpretation support. For walk-ins or urgent in-person conversations, the school designates an “interpreter of the day”—a rotating bilingual staff member available to support immediate translation needs, particularly in Spanish. For other languages, Language Line or the DOE’s T\&I Unit are used to ensure access. Through these systems, P.S. 014 ensures that families remain informed, respected, and actively involved in decision-making about their child’s safety and wellbeing—regardless of language background. By integrating bilingual staff, pre-translated resources, interpreter services, and technology platforms, the school removes language as a barrier during moments when clear, compassionate, and immediate communication matters most. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 208 of 292 Part D: Staff Awareness Part D: Staff Awareness Staff Awareness Plan to ensure staff awareness of language access requirements and available resources. 1. Describe how your school will ensure that all staff members complete the required asynchronous NYCPS Language Access Training for School-based Staff on the WeLearnNYC learning platform, and are familiar with the Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents and Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents. Additionally, explain how your Galaxy-designated LAC will disseminate future language access resources and updates to all staff members. At P.S. 014 Fairview, the leadership team is committed to ensuring that **all staff members understand and implement the NYCPS Language Access Policy** by completing the **required asynchronous Language Access Training for School-based Staff** via the **WeLearnNYC platform**. To ensure full participation, the Assistant Principal oversees a structured implementation plan aligned with NYCPS compliance expectations. The training is first introduced during a staff meeting at the start of the school year, where the purpose, expectations, and deadline for completion are clearly communicated. All staff—including teachers, paraprofessionals, related service providers, office personnel, and school aides—are given printed instructions and a direct link to the WeLearnNYC module. Time is allocated during scheduled professional learning or extended time meetings to support staff in completing the course. Completion data is monitored by the **Galaxy-designated Language Access Coordinator (LAC)**, who keeps a centralized tracking sheet to ensure 100% compliance by the DOE’s required deadline. In addition to the training, staff receive guidance on how to apply the **Best Practices for When to Provide Translation of Critical Written Communications to Parents** and the **Best Practices for When to Provide Interpretation for Critical Oral Communications with Parents**. These guidelines are posted in high-traffic staff areas (e.g., staff room, main office) and included in digital staff handbooks and weekly bulletins. The LAC also reviews scenarios during team meetings to reinforce understanding, such as when translated letters must be used or how to access interpretation during parent calls. To ensure long-term sustainability, the Galaxy-designated LAC serves as the primary point of contact for **disseminating language access updates, tools, and DOE communications**. This includes sharing new translated templates, updated guidance, and resources from the Translation and Interpretation Unit (T\&I Unit). These updates are delivered via weekly staff emails and reinforced during monthly school-based professional development. The LAC also works closely with the Parent Coordinator to ensure that front-line staff are trained on how to respond to families requesting language support, and how to access real-time tools like **Language Line**. Through structured training, ongoing communication, and centralized oversight by the LAC, P.S. 014 ensures that every staff member is equipped with the knowledge and tools needed to uphold the NYCPS Language Access Policy and provide **equitable, respectful communication to all families**, regardless of language background. To begin, **translated signage** is prominently displayed at key entry points throughout the building, including the main entrance, main office, parent coordinator’s office, and nurse’s office. These signs—provided by the NYC DOE— clearly state that language assistance is available free of charge and encourage families to ask for help in their home language. These platforms are regularly used to send reminders about available interpretation services for parent-teacher conferences, IEP meetings, and school events. The school also incorporates language access education into its **parent engagement events**, including PTA meetings, curriculum nights, and Multilingual Learner family orientations. During these sessions, school leaders and the Parent Coordinator explicitly review the translation and interpretation resources available, demonstrate how to access them, and invite families to indicate their language preferences. Events such as **International Family DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 210 of 292 Night** also celebrate the diversity of the school community while reinforcing the importance of linguistic inclusion. Through visible signage, translated resources, technology platforms, and direct engagement at family events, P.S. 014 ensures that all parents are aware of their language rights and feel empowered to request support in their home language. This approach supports stronger family-school partnerships and affirms the school’s commitment to equitable communication. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 211 of 292 Part F: Monitoring Part F: Monitoring Monitoring Methods for soliciting and implementing feedback on language assistance services. 1. In addition to the feedback received from parents via OLA’s Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey, describe the methods your school will use to gather feedback from parents regarding the language assistance services they receive in their preferred language. In addition to the official **Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey** administered by the **NYC DOE Office of Language Access (OLA)**, P.S. 014 Fairview employs multiple school-based methods to gather ongoing, authentic feedback from parents regarding the quality and accessibility of the **language assistance services** they receive. First, the **Parent Coordinator** facilitates informal check-ins during key school events—such as **Parent- Teacher Conferences, IEP meetings, and family workshops**—to ask families whether they felt supported in their preferred language and whether they experienced any barriers to communication. Feedback is documented and shared with the school’s Language Access Coordinator (LAC) to identify trends or gaps that need to be addressed. Additionally, the school administers its own **biannual parent engagement survey**, which includes targeted questions about language access, such as: * “Were materials sent home in a language you understand?” * “Was interpretation available when needed at school events or meetings?” * “How satisfied are you with the language support provided by the school?” This survey is translated into all applicable home languages using the DOE’s Translation and Interpretation Unit to ensure high participation and equitable access. To ensure that multilingual families feel comfortable expressing concerns, the school also places a **Language Access Feedback Box** near the main office, where parents can submit comments anonymously in their home language. Bilingual staff members review submissions and bring them to the attention of the Assistant Principal and LAC for follow-up. Finally, during **Multilingual Learner (ML) orientation sessions**, the Parent Coordinator facilitates **focus groups** with parents of ELLs and former ELLs, creating a safe space for families to share feedback about translation, interpretation, and overall school communication practices. These sessions not only gather insights but also build trust with families who may feel hesitant to raise concerns in formal settings. By combining DOE survey tools with local, school-based practices such as surveys, focus groups, anonymous feedback opportunities, and one-on-one conversations, P.S. 014 ensures that language access services are continually refined based on direct input from the families they are designed to support. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 212 of 292 2. How has your school worked to improve its language assistance services? Share any feedback results and outline your plans for further improvements in the upcoming school year. P.S. 014 Fairview has made deliberate and sustained efforts to improve the quality, responsiveness, and visibility of its **language assistance services**, in direct response to both formal and informal feedback from families. These efforts are aligned with the NYCPS **Language Access Policy** and the school’s commitment to equitable communication for all families, particularly those who speak a language other than English. Over the past school year, feedback collected from the **OLA Translation & Interpretation Feedback Survey**, as well as school-based family engagement surveys and parent focus groups, revealed several areas of strength and growth. Parents consistently reported satisfaction with the **availability of bilingual staff (particularly Spanish- speaking staff)** and the clarity of translated flyers and forms. However, they also expressed a need for: * **More consistent use of interpretation at school events**, especially during Q\&A segments; * **Quicker turnaround for translated IEP documents and assessment notifications**; * Better awareness of how to access interpretation over the phone or in-person for unexpected visits or emergencies. In response to this feedback, the school implemented several key improvements: * **Language Line training** was provided to all front-facing staff, including main office personnel, the nurse, and classroom teachers, to ensure consistent and immediate access to telephonic interpretation. * A bilingual **"How to Ask for an Interpreter" visual guide** was created and posted in the main office, classrooms, and nurse’s office. * The Parent Coordinator increased the use of **translated messaging via Operoo and Class Dojo**, ensuring that weekly updates and emergency alerts were consistently delivered in families’ preferred languages. * The Special Education team developed a **tracking system** for IEP translation requests and timelines to ensure that parents received translated documents in a timely manner, in accordance with DOE policy. Looking ahead to the upcoming school year, P.S. 014 plans to strengthen its language access systems by: * **Expanding language access training** to include paraprofessionals and related service providers; * Creating a **Language Access Corner** on the school website, where families can access translated resources, request interpretation support, and learn about their rights; * Hosting quarterly **Family Language Access Listening Sessions**, where multilingual families can share their experiences and offer suggestions in a structured, supportive setting; * Monitoring **translation request logs and usage of interpreter services** to identify any service gaps and streamline internal communication. These improvements are rooted in the school’s ongoing commitment to ensuring that **language is never a barrier** to meaningful family engagement, informed decision-making, or equitable access to education. Through regular feedback cycles, professional development, and increased visibility of language assistance options, P.S. 014 continues to build a school culture where every family—regardless of language—feels welcomed, informed, and empowered.
