The Title III supplemental instructional services must be based on student need. These supplemental services should complement core bilingual and ENL services required under CR Part 154. Direct supplemental services should be provided for before school, after school, and Saturday programs. Teachers providing the services must be bilingual education and/or English for Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) certified teachers. High quality professional development that is "of sufficient intensity and duration to have a positive and lasting impact on the teachers’ performance in classrooms." Professional development activities should be well-planned, ongoing events rather than one-day or short-term workshops and conferences. Parent engagement and supports must ensure that there are appropriate translation and interpretation services to meet community needs. These are in addition to mandated activities, such as parent orientation during ELL identification process or annual ELL parent meetings. Note: The Title III ELL program planning ratio is as follows: 60% to direct instruction, 10% to professional development (if funded using Title III ELL funds), 10% to parent engagement (if funded using Title III ELL funds), and 20% to OTPS (if funded using Title III ELL funds). For more information on Title III requirements, please see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. Before-School ENL Enrichment Program (Title III Funded Offering) The Before-School ENL Enrichment Program is a targeted supplemental service funded through Title III designed to accelerate English language development for Multilingual Learners (MLs) and English Language Learners (ELLs) in Grades 1–5. The program operates from 7:15 a.m. to 8:00 a.m., Monday through Friday, beginning in November 2025 and running through April 2026. This offering is designed to provide daily, structured language-development instruction that supplements—not replaces—core ENL and classroom instruction. Students are grouped according to their 2025 NYSESLAT proficiency levels (EN, EM, TR, EX). Instruction focuses explicitly on the development of academic language through targeted lessons in vocabulary development, syntax and sentence construction, morphology, reading comprehension strategies, and oral language fluency, with opportunities for guided practice and corrective feedback. Instruction is delivered in English, with strategic and intentional use of students’ home languages for scaffolding and meaning-making. Curriculum materials include Ready, Set, Go! Newcomers Kits, Practice Exercises in Basic English, Foundational Skills Toolkits, and NY TEAM resources to ensure rigorous, research-based instruction. Lessons are designed to reinforce the domains of listening, speaking, reading, and writing, while also supporting the language demands of current classroom units and upcoming assessments. The program is staffed by ENL-certified teachers who provide a combination of whole-group mini lessons, small- group skill work, and differentiated learning centers. Teachers utilize flexible grouping to respond to real-time data and student needs. The program is supervised by the Assistant Principal, who oversees implementation, ensures instructional alignment to language objectives, and monitors attendance and compliance using a shared tracking system. Formative assessments—including exit tickets, language probes, and small-group performance tasks—are used weekly to monitor progress, adjust groupings, and determine next instructional steps. The purpose of the program is to provide early-morning, low-ratio, high-frequency support that accelerates language acquisition and prepares MLs/ELLs for success during the full instructional day. Saturday ELL Academy (Title III Funded Offering) The Saturday ELL Academy is a free supplemental enrichment program in English Language Arts and Mathematics designed for MLs/ELLs in Grades 2–5 who scored at the Entering (EN), Emerging (EM), Transitioning (TR), or Expanding (EX) levels on the 2025 NYSESLAT. The program runs from November 2025 to April 2026, operating on Saturdays from 8:00 a.m. to 11:00 a.m. Instruction is delivered fully in English with targeted support in students’ L1 to promote comprehension and cross-linguistic transfer. Students are grouped by proficiency level, with each grade having one EN/EM section and one TR/EX section. Instruction incorporates whole-group lessons and small-group instruction through co-teaching, station rotation, and DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 217 of 292 flexible groupings. All instruction is aligned to language objectives tailored to students’ developmental needs. Curriculum priorities include semantics, pragmatics, syntax, morphology, phonics and phonemic awareness, and academic vocabulary. Mathematics instruction is embedded for Grades 3–5 to prepare students for the State Math Assessments. Materials include Practice Exercises in Basic English, Phonics and Word Study resources, Ready, Set, Go! Newcomers Kits, and TEAM Toolkits. The Saturday Academy is staffed by at least thirteen ENL or Bilingual-certified teachers, supported by three content-area teachers. An ENL coordinator provides push-in coaching and ensures instructional alignment. An Assistant Principal supervises on-site operations, instruction, and parent engagement, supported by a school aide who manages attendance and documentation. Attendance is recorded daily through a shared Google Doc. Funding from Title III and school-based allocations supports staffing, curriculum materials, supervision, and parent engagement opportunities. Fiscal oversight is managed by the Assistant Principal to ensure compliance and appropriate use of Title III funds. How ELLs will be grouped for instruction and specify the number of student groups. Rationale for Title III ELL Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III ELL funded program. For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program The 24Q014 Title III (Immigrant) program for 2025 is designed to ensure that immigrant students at P.S. 14 receive comprehensive academic and cultural supports that foster both English language proficiency and social integration into American schools. This rationale is grounded in multiple data sources, including Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results, ELA State Exam performance trends, the ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT), and school-level needs assessments. These data collectively highlight the critical need for targeted interventions and supplemental programming under Title III funding. English Language Acquisition (ELA): The program emphasizes growth in all four language domains—reading, writing, speaking, and listening—through explicit language instruction embedded in both ELA and Math. Saturday ELL Academy serves as the primary vehicle for Title III-funded instruction, giving students an additional platform to develop conversational English while acquiring academic vocabulary. Instruction is designed for small-group and whole-group contexts, with students grouped according to their Spring 2025 NYSESLAT performance levels (EN, EM, TR, EX). Data Justification – NYSESLAT 2025: Analysis of the 2025 NYSESLAT indicates that while progress was made, significant needs remain: 51.79% of MLs/ELLs advanced at least one proficiency level (347 out of 670 students), leaving nearly half stagnating at their previous level. A total of 162 students reached Commanding (including those scoring both Commanding + ELA 3/4), but this still represents 26% of the ELL population. EDAT subgroup analysis reveals that Entering and Emerging students remain the largest cohorts, underscoring the ongoing need for newcomer-focused language development. Data Justification – ELA State Exams 2025: The achievement gap in ELA remains significant: Grade 3: 15.1% scored at Levels 3–4 DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 219 of 292 Grade 4: 11.4% scored at Levels 3–4 Grade 5: 14.9% scored at Levels 3–4 These results confirm that immigrant students continue to perform well below citywide averages, requiring intensified support in academic language and literacy. Program Design and Implementation: Saturday ELL Academy, funded through Title III Immigration allocations, responds directly to these data points. Classes are organized by proficiency band (EN/EM and TR/EX for each grade) to tailor instruction. Instruction integrates language and content learning, with teachers employing scaffolding, visuals, and L1 supports to make grade-level content accessible. Beyond academics, the program emphasizes social and cultural integration, with collaborative activities, parent workshops, and cultural literacy embedded into weekly sessions. Supervision and Funding: The program is staffed by ENL/Bilingual-certified teachers and overseen by an Assistant Principal and ENL coordinator, with attendance and compliance documented through shared digital systems. Funding is secured through Title III Immigration allocations, with expenditures covering teacher stipends, curriculum resources (Continental Press, TEAM Toolkits, newcomer kits), supervision, and parent engagement programming. Ongoing Monitoring and Evaluation: Program monitoring relies on multiple data sources to measure impact: Formative and summative assessments aligned with NYSESLAT standards. Teacher observations and low-inference notes, ensuring fidelity of instructional delivery. Student/parent surveys to evaluate cultural integration and program satisfaction. EDAT progress tracking, monitoring growth across subgroups (Entering, Emerging, Transitioning, Expanding, Commanding). Post-program analysis will compare pre- and post-assessment results, attendance data, and State assessment outcomes to measure effectiveness. Findings will inform program modifications, resource allocation, and targeted professional development for staff. By explicitly tying Title III programming to NYSESLAT proficiency data, ELA State test outcomes, and EDAT subgroup analysis, the 24Q014 Title III (Immigrant) program demonstrates a strong, data-driven rationale. The evidence clearly supports the continued need for Saturday ELL Academy as a vital intervention that addresses persistent achievement gaps, builds academic English proficiency, and fosters cultural integration for immigrant students. Developing ELLs (Transitioning/Expanding): Students progressing through intermediate proficiency who require scaffolded academic language support to access grade-level content. ELLs with IEPs: Students who require both language acquisition support and specialized instruction to meet their mandated services. Instruction integrates language development with content mastery, while also embedding strategies that support ELLs with IEPs through collaboration with Special Education staff. By deliberately structuring both the Early Bird and Saturday programs around grade level, proficiency band, and subgroup needs, the Title III program ensures equity of access and targeted interventions that align with state mandates and directly respond to P.S. 14’s NYSESLAT and ELA achievement data. At P.S. 014 Fairview, the implementation of Title III ELL-funded programming is guided by a structured and equity- driven approach that ensures both family engagement and full compliance with fiscal and programmatic requirements. Strong systems for record keeping are in place to safeguard transparency, prepare for potential audits, and align with the expectations outlined on page 128 of the NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide for Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners (PRG). Family Notification and Documentation: At the start of each program cycle, families are notified about the availability and purpose of Title III-funded programs through translated letters in their preferred language. Notifications are distributed via backpack mail, Operoo, Class Dojo, and posted on the school website. The Parent Coordinator and ML Compliance Lead maintain distribution logs, and signed copies of translated letters are retained. For audit purposes, hard copies are filed in the school’s central Title III compliance binder, while digital versions and screenshots of online postings are archived on a secure shared drive. Attendance and Participation Records: Attendance is tracked for every Title III-funded session, including Early Bird and Saturday Academy. Session facilitators submit daily attendance logs that are cross-referenced with ATS to ensure accuracy. Attendance is monitored for patterns of participation, which inform targeted outreach to families. These records are collected, signed, and stored in both physical binders and digital folders under the supervision of the Assistant Principal and Title III Program Coordinator. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 225 of 292 Staff Certification Records: To ensure compliance with certification requirements, the school maintains a binder containing copies of ENL/Bilingual teacher certifications, licenses, and assignments. These are verified and updated regularly by the Title III Program Coordinator. Only appropriately certified staff are assigned to Title III-funded instructional roles, with records available for immediate audit review. Parent and Community Engagement Documentation: Every parent workshop or family literacy event funded by Title III is fully documented with copies of multilingual flyers, agendas, sign-in sheets, and feedback forms. Materials used during sessions (e.g., slide decks, translated resources, handouts) are retained and archived. Feedback is reviewed to refine programming, and documentation is maintained in the Title III binder and digital archives. Professional Development Records: When Title III funds are used to support professional learning, the school archives agendas, sign-in sheets, materials, and reflective notes. PD sessions focused on supporting Long-Term ELLs, CR-SE, and ENL best practices are documented and reviewed annually for compliance with program objectives. Fiscal Documentation: Expenditures of Title III funds are meticulously documented. The school maintains an audit-ready binder containing purchase orders, invoices, payroll per-session timecards, receipts, and vendor contracts tied to Title III allocations. The Title III Program Coordinator collaborates with the school’s finance secretary to reconcile all expenditures and submit required fiscal documentation to central offices. To ensure compliance, no more than 20% of Title III funds are allocated to materials purchases, consistent with PRG guidelines. Audit-Ready Systems: All Title III documentation is organized using a dual system of hard-copy binders and digital shared drives. Each binder is divided into clearly labeled sections: Family Notifications Attendance Logs Staff Certifications Parent Engagement Events Professional Development Fiscal Records This organizational structure ensures that all records are audit-ready, accessible, and consistent with DOE expectations as detailed in the PRG. Through these robust systems for multilingual communication, instructional oversight, and precise record keeping, P.S. 014 ensures that its Title III ELL program operates with fidelity. The alignment to page 128 of the PRG demonstrates the school’s commitment not only to compliance, but also to maintaining the highest standards of accountability, transparency, and support for multilingual learners and their families. P.S. 14 is dedicated to providing comprehensive professional development to equip all staff members with the skills and knowledge needed to effectively support English Language Learners (ELLs). Here's a breakdown of the PD activities by topic: 1. Effective ELL Instructional Strategies: Differentiated Instruction: Teachers will learn how to differentiate instruction to meet the diverse learning needs of ELLs, considering their language proficiency levels, cultural backgrounds, and learning styles. Scaffolding: PD will focus on practical strategies for scaffolding instruction, providing temporary support to help ELLs access challenging content and gradually increase their independence. Language Acquisition Theories: Teachers will gain an understanding of language acquisition theories and how they apply to ELL instruction, informing their teaching practices and approaches. Content-Based Language Instruction: PD will explore content-based language instruction, integrating language and content learning to make learning more engaging and meaningful for ELLs. Technology Integration: Teachers will learn how to effectively integrate technology to enhance ELL instruction, using tools and resources to support language development, provide differentiated practice, and promote student engagement. 2. Assessment and Evaluation: Identifying ELL Student Needs: PD will focus on strategies for assessing ELL students' language proficiency, academic strengths and weaknesses, and learning styles to inform instruction and intervention. Formative and Summative Assessments: Teachers will learn how to use a variety of formative and summative assessments to monitor ELL student progress and evaluate their learning outcomes. Progress Monitoring: PD will cover effective progress monitoring techniques to track ELL student growth over time and make data-driven instructional decisions. Interpreting Data: Teachers will develop skills in analyzing and interpreting assessment data to understand ELL student needs, identify areas for improvement, and adjust instruction accordingly. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 227 of 292 3. Culturally Responsive Teaching: Building Relationships: PD will emphasize the importance of building strong relationships with ELL students and families, creating a welcoming and inclusive classroom environment. Understanding Diverse Cultural Backgrounds: Teachers will learn about the diverse cultural backgrounds of their ELL students, developing cultural sensitivity and awareness to avoid cultural misunderstandings and biases. Incorporating Home Languages and Experiences: PD will explore strategies for valuing and incorporating ELL students' home languages and cultural experiences into instruction, promoting a sense of belonging and identity. 4. Collaboration and Communication: Working Effectively with Other Educators: PD will foster collaboration among teachers, ESL specialists, support staff, and administrators to create a cohesive and supportive learning environment for ELLs. Parent Communication: Teachers will learn effective strategies for communicating with ELL parents, including using interpreters, providing translated materials, and utilizing culturally sensitive communication practices. Community Engagement: PD will explore ways to connect with community organizations and resources that can support ELL students and their families. 5. Specific Needs of Different ELL Subgroups: Newcomers: Teachers will receive training on specific strategies for supporting newcomer ELLs, including addressing their unique social and emotional needs and providing intensive language support. Struggling Learners: PD will address effective interventions and support for struggling ELLs, including identifying learning difficulties and providing targeted instruction. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. Program Description – Early Bird The Early Bird Program is a free supplemental enrichment initiative for Grades 2–5, targeting students who scored at Entering (EN), Emerging (EM), Transitioning (TR), and Expanding (EX) on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT. The program prioritizes Newcomer and developing ELLs/MLs, offering intensive instruction in semantics, pragmatics, syntax, phonics, and morphology to accelerate foundational literacy and oral language skills. Instruction is delivered in English with L1 supports, preparing students for success on the Spring 2026 NYSESLAT and grade-level content. Program Description – Saturday Academy Saturday Academy extends learning opportunities for Grades 3–5, integrating ELA and Math instruction with a strong emphasis on academic vocabulary and comprehension of math word problems. Classes are grouped by grade level and proficiency band (EN/EM and TR/EX). Sessions are taught through co-teaching, station rotations, and flexible grouping, giving students tailored support in both language development and content mastery. Program Description – After-School The After-School Program supplements Early Bird and Saturday offerings by providing homework support, fluency practice, and targeted small-group interventions. Students are grouped by proficiency level using NYSESLAT data, with a focus on reinforcing academic vocabulary, reading fluency, and oral participation. Rationale for Title III Immigrant Funded Programs Data-Driven Justification NYSESLAT 2025 Results: Out of 670 ELLs, 347 students (51.79%) advanced at least one proficiency level, while nearly half stagnated. Only 62 students reached Commanding, including those earning both Commanding + ELA 3/4 scores — representing under 10% of the ELL population. The largest subgroup remains Newcomers (EN/EM: 519 students), followed by Developing ELLs (TR/EX: 133 students). This confirms that the majority of students are still in need of foundational or intermediate language development. ELA State Assessments 2025: Achievement gaps persist, with only 15.1% of Grade 3, 11.4% of Grade 4, and 14.9% of Grade 5 ELLs scoring at Levels 3–4, compared to significantly higher citywide averages. Math Assessments 2025: Persistent gaps remain in language-heavy domains such as multi-step word problems. Teacher reports confirm that ELLs struggle more with vocabulary comprehension than with computation skills. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 230 of 292 Teacher Surveys and Observations: Educators report challenges with scaffolding academic vocabulary, using L1 strategically, and differentiating for newcomers. Observations highlight inconsistencies in CR-SE practices, further justifying targeted PD. Program Outcome Data: Previous cycles of Saturday Academy demonstrated measurable growth in listening and reading domains, validating the effectiveness of small-group targeted interventions. However, gaps remain in writing and math vocabulary, requiring PD alignment. Professional Development (PD) Plan for Title III Programs Alignment of PD to Program-Specific Needs Early Bird Program: Data indicates the EN/EM subgroup (519 students) struggles most with phonics, decoding, and oral fluency. PD will focus on systematic phonics instruction, scaffolding for newcomers, and oral language development strategies. Saturday Academy: The TR/EX subgroup (133 students) shows persistent gaps in ELA and Math vocabulary. PD will emphasize math language development, building academic vocabulary banks, and integrating scaffolding in higher- order comprehension tasks. After-School Program: Observations confirm uneven use of culturally responsive practices. PD will provide training on using cognates, integrating culturally relevant texts, and leveraging home language supports to reinforce after- school literacy and engagement. PD Focus Areas (SY 2025–2026): Formative and summative assessment strategies to measure growth across all programs. Scaffolding and differentiation for EN, EM, TR, and EX groups. Academic vocabulary development in both ELA and Math contexts. Strategies for supporting newcomers’ survival English and cultural acclimation. Integration of CR-SE practices to strengthen engagement and inclusivity. Impact on Teachers and Students Teachers will gain confidence in addressing ELL subgroups through targeted scaffolds, differentiated instruction, and language-content integration. Students will demonstrate measurable gains on pre-/post-assessments, NYSESLAT domains, and classroom performance. Consistent application of PD strategies will narrow gaps in ELA comprehension and Math word problem performance, while increasing engagement and participation across all Title III offerings. Professional development is sole used for the Title III program and teachers who support the program. The professional development (PD) plan at P.S. 14 is designed as an ongoing cycle of learning, with monthly sessions, collaborative planning meetings, peer observations, and coaching cycles embedded across the school year. Rather than one-time workshops, PD is aligned directly to the Title III program offerings (Early Bird, Saturday Academy, After-School) and is responsive to student needs identified through baseline, formative, and summative assessments, including mCLASS, SAAVAS, NEWA, NYSITELL, and NYSESLAT. Alignment of PD to Title III Programs Early Bird Program (Grades 2–5, EN/EM focus): PD sessions will emphasize phonemic awareness, decoding strategies, oral fluency, and guided reading routines. Teachers will receive training on systematic phonics interventions and the use of bilingual scaffolds to accelerate newcomer language development. Saturday Academy (Grades 3–5, TR/EX focus): PD will focus on academic vocabulary development, amplification of reading to meet grade-level standards, and strategies for embedding language into math problem-solving. Teachers will engage in collaborative lesson study cycles to design language-rich ELA and math lessons aligned to State standards. After-School Program (Grades 2–5, reinforcement): PD will emphasize culturally responsive teaching practices, leveraging home languages, and crash-course activities in speaking, listening, reading, and writing to support daily homework help and small-group interventions. Ongoing PD Focus Areas Effective ELL Instructional Strategies: Differentiated instruction, scaffolding for EN/EM and TR/EX groups, content- based language instruction, and technology integration to promote language development. Assessment and Evaluation: Using formative and summative assessments (mCLASS, NYSESLAT checkpoints) to identify ELL needs, track growth, and adjust instruction in real time. Culturally Responsive Teaching: Building strong relationships with ELL students and families, incorporating students’ home languages into instruction, and embedding diverse texts that reflect students’ identities. Collaboration and Co-Teaching: Strengthening ENL and content teacher partnerships through lesson planning, co- teaching models (team teaching, station rotation), and peer coaching cycles. ELL Subgroup-Specific Needs: Targeted strategies for newcomers, long-term ELLs, students with disabilities, and advanced learners to ensure access to rigorous grade-level content. Compliance and Standards: Continuous review of ESSA, NYSED ELL standards, CR-SE framework, and NYC DOE Policy & Reference Guide expectations to ensure program fidelity. Program-Specific Skill Development Phonemic Awareness & Fluency: Early Bird teachers engage in PD cycles on structured literacy and fluency practice. Guided Reading & Academic Vocabulary: Saturday Academy PD focuses on building vocabulary banks and guided reading protocols aligned to NYSESLAT standards. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 233 of 292 Speaking, Listening, Writing: After-School PD emphasizes short, interactive activities that foster productive oral and written language use. Math for ELLs: Across all programs, PD includes strategies for accessing math through language scaffolds, visuals, and Illustrative Math tasks tailored for ELLs. Sustainability and Monitoring PD sessions are held monthly, supplemented by weekly ENL/content planning meetings. Teachers participate in learning cycles that include modeling, classroom implementation, observation, and reflection. Feedback is gathered through teacher surveys, PD exit tickets, and student performance data, ensuring PD is responsive and iterative. By ensuring that PD is ongoing, program-aligned, and data-driven, P.S. 14 equips teachers with the tools needed to accelerate English acquisition, leverage home language supports, and promote high achievement in core academic areas. This sustained professional learning directly supports the goals of Title III Immigrant-funded programming. Professional development is sole used for the Title III program and teachers who support the program. Revised Narrative: Rationale for Family Engagement Workshops The rationale for family workshops at P.S. 14 is to empower parents of ELLs with the knowledge and skills necessary to support their children’s academic, social, and emotional development. Research and school-based data confirm that parental involvement is a key factor in ELL student success, and equipping families with strategies to engage in their child’s learning strengthens home–school partnerships. Workshops will serve to: Enhance communication and collaboration by bridging the gap between home and school. Improve academic performance by familiarizing parents with curriculum, instructional methods, and strategies to support learning at home. Promote social-emotional well-being (SEL) by equipping families with tools to build resilience and address the emotional needs of ELL students. Increase engagement by offering culturally and linguistically responsive topics aligned with the needs and interests of families. Data Sources Used to Plan Activities The design of these workshops is grounded in multiple data sources to ensure that activities respond to authentic family needs: Parent Surveys: Formal surveys were distributed in English, Spanish, Arabic, and Chinese to capture parent preferences regarding workshop topics (e.g., homework support, navigating U.S. schools, SEL). Survey results revealed strong interest in literacy support at home, state testing information, and social-emotional development strategies. In-House Informal Surveys: ENL teachers and the Parent Coordinator collected feedback during parent-teacher conferences and daily interactions. Parents frequently requested clearer guidance on supporting math vocabulary and reading fluency at home. PTA/PA Requests: Parent Association meeting notes documented requests for information sessions on bilingual resources, graduation requirements, and college readiness, which were factored into the workshop plan. Teacher Input: ENL and classroom teachers noted that students with lower attendance and weaker academic performance often had families seeking more direct guidance, leading to targeted SEL-focused sessions. Student Data: Analysis of Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results (with 519 EN/EM students and 133 TR/EX students still below proficiency) and ELA State Test performance (Grades 3–5 ELLs averaging only 11–15% at Levels 3–4) highlighted the urgent need for family workshops on language development strategies and literacy reinforcement. Community Resources: Partnerships with local immigrant-serving agencies provided additional insights into family needs, including legal rights in schools, bilingual library access, and social-emotional supports for newcomers. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 239 of 292 By triangulating data from surveys, teacher feedback, parent requests, student outcomes, and community partnerships, P.S. 14 ensures that family workshops are not generic, but tailored to the needs of immigrant and ELL families. Flyers will be translated into Spanish, Arabic, and Chinese prior to distribution. Facilitation: Sessions will be led primarily by ENL staff and the Parent Coordinator, with translation services provided. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 247 of 292 Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Application for Immigrant Funding REQUIREMENTS Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds can be used for activities that provide enhanced instructional opportunities for immigrant students, which include: Identification and acquisition of curricular materials, educational software and technologies to be used in the program. Family literacy, parent outreach and training activities designed to assist parents in becoming active participants in the education of their children and understand their rights and the benefits of multilingualism. Support for personnel, including teacher aides, parent coordinators, and school counselors who have been specifically trained, or are being trained, to provide services to immigrant students and families. Provision of tutorials, mentoring, and academic or career counseling for immigrant students, as well as engagement in pre-college and work-based learning experiences. Basic instructional services directly attributable to the aforementioned school districts, including costs for additional classroom supplies, transportation costs, or other costs directly attributable to such services. Other instructional services that are designed to assist immigrant students to achieve in elementary schools and secondary schools in the United States, such as programs of introduction to the educational system and civics education. School activities, coordinated with community-based organizations, institutions of higher education, private sector entities, or other entities with expertise in working with immigrants, to assist families of immigrant students by offering comprehensive community services. Funding follows similar guidelines for Title III funds established by the Office of Multilingual Learners (OML) and can only be used to provide supplementary services to immigrant students, who may or may not be English Language Learners (ELLs). Please note: Immigrant students are not necessarily English Language Learners (ELLs), and ELLs may or may not be immigrants. For the purpose of this funding, immigrant students are defined as individuals who were not born in any U.S. state (this includes the District of Columbia and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico); and have not been attending one or more schools in any one or more states for more than three full, academic years (the months need not be consecutive). DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 248 of 292 Priority areas for the use of Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds are as follows: Extended day programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to provide targeted, small-group instruction, credit recovery support, and postsecondary preparation. Implementing strong student supports to increase graduation rates. Community-based and school-based programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to strengthen partnerships between immigrant families and schools. Developing new and/or enhancing existing programs for newcomers (meeting criteria defined above). Supplementing the opening or growth of Transitional Bilingual Education programs and/or Dual Language Bilingual Education programs serving immigrant students. Professional learning on meeting the needs of immigrant students, creating a welcoming and inclusive school environment, and effective instructional practices. For more information on Title III requirements, refer to NYSED’s guidance on allowable and unallowable expenditures for Title III. For specific allocation amounts per school, see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. DIRECTIONS Please complete the application below. Schools must use the funds to support their immigrant students by developing a plan that has each of the following components: Direct instruction, supplemental program for immigrant students, Professional development that bolsters the instructional practice of teachers of immigrant students, and Family engagement activities that support families of immigrant students. Completed applications must be submitted in iPlan for review and approval. Please refer to the respective School Allocation Memo. Schools are not permitted to use funds until they submit a plan and receive notification of its approval. Program Timing The Early Bird Program will run before school, from 7:05 a.m. to 8:05 a.m., Monday through Friday, beginning January 2026 and continuing through April 2026. The program will operate for approximately 16 weeks and will be housed in designated classrooms at P.S. 14Q, serving newcomer and developing English Language Learners (ELLs), including recently arrived immigrant students. Activities and Enhancement of Instructional Opportunities The Early Bird Program continues to serve as a targeted supplemental instructional initiative that extends learning DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 251 of 292 opportunities for immigrant and newcomer ELLs beyond the regular school day. The program’s instructional design will support language acquisition, academic vocabulary, and grade-level content access through the following components: Phonemic Awareness and Fluency Building: Daily structured exercises focusing on phonics, pronunciation, vocabulary expansion, and reading fluency. Guided Reading and Writing Workshops: Targeted literacy sessions reinforcing comprehension strategies, sentence development, and grammar practice through high-interest texts. Academic Speaking and Listening Activities: Interactive dialogues, role-play, and oral presentations to strengthen academic discourse and confidence in English communication. Math Through Language: Scaffolded math instruction emphasizing vocabulary, problem-solving, and concept connections using visuals and manipulatives. Integration of Realia and Technology: Use of real-life objects, digital supports (e.g., iReady, Imagine Learning), and culturally relevant texts to promote engagement and comprehension. Students will participate in interactive, scaffolded lessons that integrate ELA, mathematics, and science while receiving bilingual support in their home language (L1) when appropriate. All instructional activities are designed to accelerate English proficiency while maintaining content rigor and fostering inclusion. Grouping of Students Students will be grouped by English proficiency levels using the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT and initial home language survey data for new arrivals. Groupings will ensure differentiation and equitable instructional access. Grouping Structure: Grade 2: 1 Group of 10 Students (Emerging and Transitioning levels). Grade 3: 3 Group of 10 students each (Emerging and Transitioning levels). Grade 4: 1 Group of 10 Students each (Emerging and Transitioning levels). Grade 5: 1 Group of 10 students each (Emerging, Transitioning, and Expanding levels). Total Number of Groups: 6 Total Number of Students: 60 Number of ELLs per Group: 10 Grouping will remain flexible throughout the cycle, with students regrouped based on ongoing assessment data to meet evolving linguistic and academic needs. Instructional Delivery Instruction will be delivered through collaborative, research-based co-teaching models aligned to the Danielson Framework and NYSED CR-SE principles: Team Teaching: ENL and content area teachers co-plan and co-deliver integrated lessons blending language and content standards. Parallel Teaching: Both teachers simultaneously deliver instruction to smaller groups to enhance differentiation and engagement. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 252 of 292 Station Rotation: Students rotate through language, reading, and content-based learning centers emphasizing oral language, literacy, and applied skills. Flexible Grouping: Teachers use formative data to modify groupings, ensuring ongoing alignment to individual student goals and proficiency levels. This multi-modal approach promotes engagement, reduces linguistic barriers, and provides personalized pathways for student success. Supervision and Cost The program will be supervised by the Assistant Principal for ENL and Bilingual Programs, ensuring fidelity to Title III Immigrant compliance and instructional alignment. Supervisor: Assistant Principal (regular day ends 2:50 p.m.) Per Session Hours: 7:05–8:05 a.m., five days per week (Title III Immigrant–funded supervision) Supervisory Duties: Oversight of attendance, instructional quality, alignment to Title III objectives, and teacher coordination. Staffing includes: 8 ENL/Bilingual-certified teachers 4 content area teachers with training in ELL differentiation 1 paraprofessional/school aide for logistical and attendance support All staffing, supervision, and instructional materials will be funded through Title III Immigrant allocations. Assessment and Monitoring Student progress will be continuously monitored through multiple measures, including: Pre- and post-assessments in literacy and oral language to determine growth. Ongoing formative assessments (running records, oral check-ins, exit tickets). Teacher-created rubrics to evaluate student progress in listening, speaking, reading, and writing. Parent engagement sessions (February and April 2026) to share student progress and provide resources for home language and English support. Data will be reviewed bi-weekly by the ENL team to adjust instruction and ensure each student is on track toward achieving English language proficiency and grade-level benchmarks. Rationale for Title III Immigrant Funded Programs DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 253 of 292 Describe the rationale for each Title III Immigrant funded program: For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program The Early Bird Program serves as a targeted intervention for immigrant English Language Learners (ELLs), addressing persistent linguistic and academic barriers identified through comprehensive quantitative performance data and qualitative instructional analysis. The program specifically supports immigrant students in Grades 2–5 performing at the Entering (EN), Emerging (EM), Transitioning (TR), and Expanding (EX) proficiency levels on the Spring 2025 NYSESLAT. Quantitative Findings Analysis of Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results indicated that 65 immigrant students (43%) in Grades 2–5 scored at the Entering (EN) level and 52 immigrant students (35%) scored at the Emerging (EM) level. Combined, 78% of immigrant ELLs remain at the two lowest proficiency levels, demonstrating significant needs in phonemic awareness, academic vocabulary, and reading comprehension. State assessment data from the 2024–2025 administration revealed that 41% of immigrant ELLs scored at Level 1 on the ELA exam and 38% scored at Level 1 in Mathematics, compared to 18% and 15% respectively among non-ELL peers. These gaps highlight persistent disparities in language-mediated academic tasks, particularly in reading comprehension and multi-step word problems. EDAT subgroup analysis showed that immigrant ELLs in the lowest proficiency bands demonstrated an average student growth percentile (SGP) of 32 in ELA and 29 in Math, falling below the NYC DOE benchmark of 50 for expected annual growth. Newcomer students within this group posted the lowest growth rates. A total of 60 immigrant ELLs (40%) were identified as requiring supplemental instructional time based on combined NYSESLAT, EDAT, and classroom performance indicators. Attendance records from SY 2024–2025 indicated that 27% of newcomer immigrant students met the threshold for chronic absenteeism, compared to 14% of the general student population, correlating with slower English language acquisition and reduced instructional exposure. Identified Trends Among Immigrant Students Longitudinal analysis from 2023–2025 shows that immigrant ELLs who entered at the EN/EM levels required an average of 2.5 to 3 years to reach the Expanding (EX) level without targeted intervention, delaying access to grade- level curriculum. Students who did not participate in supplemental programming demonstrated an average NYSESLAT growth rate of 0.6 proficiency levels per year, compared to 1.2 levels per year among participants in structured before-school or after-school language programs. Immigrant students with inconsistent attendance demonstrated significantly lower gains in speaking and writing domains, reinforcing the need for consistent, structured instructional time. Newly arrived students (less than 12 months in U.S. schools) were twice as likely to score at Level 1 on state assessments as long-term ELLs, particularly in ELA. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 254 of 292 Qualitative Insights Teacher feedback and instructional team debriefs consistently identified difficulties in grade-level text comprehension, academic discourse, and mathematical reasoning in English among immigrant students. Classroom observations conducted during Fall 2025 cycles showed that immigrant ELLs demonstrated increased engagement and improved decoding and oral language skills in small, linguistically responsive instructional settings. Student voice surveys indicated that 72% of participating immigrant ELLs reported increased confidence in speaking during whole-group and small-group instruction after receiving early-morning intervention. ENL and bilingual educators emphasized that sustained, scaffolded instruction prior to the start of the school day allows for intensified language development without reducing access to core academic instruction. Data Sources Used to Justify the Need for the Program Spring 2025 NYSESLAT Results: Identified that 78% of immigrant ELLs remain at EN/EM proficiency levels, guiding instructional grouping and curriculum design. NYS ELA and Math Assessments (2024–2025): Documented that over 40% of immigrant ELLs performed at Level 1 in ELA and Math. EDAT Progress Monitoring Reports: Revealed below-benchmark growth percentiles for newcomer and long-term immigrant ELLs. Teacher Observations and Team Reflections: Confirmed persistent linguistic barriers and instructional needs. Parent Engagement Logs: Reflected strong family demand for additional language support opportunities. SY 2024–2025 Early Bird Attendance and Outcome Data: Demonstrated higher NYSESLAT growth and improved attendance among program participants. Please make sure to describe: Professional development to be provided Activities that teachers will engage with by topic Whether the professional development provided will be at cost to Title III Immigrant funds Professional Development Summary – Title III Immigrant Program (SY 2025–2026) Our Title III Immigrant professional development plan for SY 2025–2026 is designed to strengthen teacher capacity in supporting the language acquisition, academic achievement, and socio-emotional integration of immigrant and newcomer students. The sessions will ensure that ENL, bilingual, and content area teachers possess the tools necessary to provide equitable and high-quality instruction aligned with NYSED CR-SE and Danielson Framework standards. Professional Development Overview Professional development will be delivered through a series of targeted workshops, collaborative planning sessions, and coaching cycles between January and March 2026, coinciding with the Early Bird Program cycle. All sessions will be led by the Assistant Principal for ENL and Bilingual Programs and the ENL Coordinator, with additional facilitation by experienced ENL and content area teacher leaders. The professional learning series will include four key strands: Language Acquisition Strategies, Academic Content Integration, Culturally Responsive Pedagogy, and Family & Community Engagement. All teachers in the Title III program will be included in the training and Professional Development listed. Activities by Topic 1. English Language Acquisition (ELA Focus) Language Objectives & Scaffolding: DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 263 of 292 Participants will learn to write measurable language objectives aligned to content standards and differentiate instruction for multiple proficiency levels. Data-Informed Planning: Teachers will analyze Spring 2025 NYSESLAT and EDAT data to identify trends and inform small-group instruction within the Early Bird Program. 2. Academic Achievement and Content Integration Content-Specific ELL Strategies: Sessions will focus on teaching math and science through visual supports, graphic organizers, and real-world contexts to make language and content accessible. Co-Teaching and Collaborative Planning: ENL and content area teachers will plan and model co-teaching approaches (team, parallel, and station teaching) to promote shared ownership of ELL instruction. Formative Assessment for Multilingual Learners: Teachers will explore authentic assessment techniques that capture both language growth and content mastery, including oral rubrics and interactive performance tasks. 3. Culturally Responsive-Sustaining Pedagogy (CR-SE) Cultural Asset Mapping: Teachers will identify and integrate the cultural backgrounds of immigrant students into lesson planning, ensuring representation and inclusivity. Social-Emotional Learning for Immigrant Students: Sessions will emphasize SEL practices that address transition stress, identity affirmation, and peer integration for newcomer ELLs. Building Inclusive Classroom Communities: Teachers will collaborate on classroom practices that foster belonging and cultural pride while maintaining high academic expectations. 4. Family and Community Engagement Engaging Multilingual Families: Teachers will learn effective communication techniques using DOE translation resources and community partnerships to strengthen school-family collaboration. Connecting with Community Organizations: The PD will introduce teachers to local immigrant support networks and after-school programs that extend student learning beyond the classroom. Funding Source and Cost Structure All professional development activities under this initiative will be funded through Title III Immigrant allocations for SY 2025–2026. Teacher Per-Session Compensation: Teachers attending workshops outside contractual hours will be compensated at the DOE per-session rate. PD per- DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 264 of 292 session costs will remain below 10% of the total Title III Immigrant allocation. OTPS/Vendor Costs: No external vendors will be utilized this year; PD will be internally facilitated by school-based staff. Therefore, OTPS costs will remain below the 20% threshold of the Title III Immigrant budget. Format and Evaluation Formats: In-person workshops, after-school planning sessions, and asynchronous online modules. Duration: One-hour sessions, twice monthly (January–March 2026). Evaluation: Participant feedback, exit tickets, and lesson plan artifacts will be collected to measure impact and alignment to Title III goals. Documentation: Attendance sheets, agendas, materials, and reflections will be archived as evidence of compliance. Expected Outcomes Through this professional development series, teachers will: Implement research-based strategies that improve English language proficiency across all domains. Design linguistically scaffolded lessons that integrate language and content objectives. Strengthen classroom environments that affirm cultural identity and support social-emotional growth. Deepen collaboration between ENL, bilingual, and content area teachers to sustain equitable instructional practices. Rationale for Professional Development Describe the rationale for the professional development to be provided. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. For each activity include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for the professional development Data sources used to justify the professional development The Title III Immigrant professional development (PD) plan for SY 2025–2026 is grounded in a systematic analysis of student performance data, instructional observation trends, teacher feedback, and school climate indicators. Multiple data sources from SY 2024–2025 and Fall 2025 demonstrate persistent instructional and systemic barriers impacting immigrant students’ academic, linguistic, and social-emotional development. These findings directly inform the selection and design of professional learning activities. Data sources include Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results, EDAT reports, state assessments, Danielson-aligned DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 265 of 292 observations, teacher surveys (Fall 2025), student climate surveys (2025), and parent engagement records. Activity-Specific Rationale 1. English Language Acquisition (ELA Focus) A. Sheltered Instruction and Language Development Strategies Data Findings and Trends: Fall 2025 observation data aligned to Danielson Component 3c indicated that only 54% of observed lessons demonstrated consistent use of language objectives, structured academic discourse, and explicit vocabulary instruction. Teacher feedback surveys administered in October 2025 showed that 72% of classroom teachers reported limited confidence in implementing sheltered instruction strategies across content areas. Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results indicated that 42% of students in Grades 3–5 remained at the EN and EM proficiency levels, reflecting limited growth in oral language and academic discourse. PD Justification: Because observation and survey data indicate inconsistent implementation of sheltered instruction and continued low language proficiency levels, professional development will focus on explicit modeling of sheltered instruction techniques, academic language scaffolds, and structured oral language routines to improve instructional consistency and student language outcomes. B. Culturally Responsive-Sustaining Pedagogy (CR-SE) Data Findings and Trends: Student interviews conducted in September 2025 revealed that many immigrant students reported limited opportunities to connect academic content to their cultural backgrounds. The 2025 NYC School Survey indicated that only 68% of students agreed that teachers incorporate cultural examples into instruction, falling below the district average of 74%. Lesson plan reviews from Fall 2025 showed that fewer than 40% of reviewed units included culturally relevant texts or community-based examples. PD Justification: Given student feedback and curriculum audits demonstrating limited cultural representation, PD will focus on CR- SE-aligned instructional design, culturally relevant curriculum planning, and inclusive classroom discourse practices to increase student engagement and identity affirmation. C. Assessment Literacy and Data-Informed Instruction Data Findings and Trends: Teacher reflection surveys (November 2025) indicated that 65% of teachers reported difficulty interpreting NYSESLAT and EDAT reports for instructional planning. EDAT progress monitoring data showed that 28% of multilingual learners had incomplete or inconsistent growth documentation between winter and spring benchmarks. Team meeting notes revealed inconsistent use of formative assessment data during instructional planning cycles. PD Justification: Because teachers demonstrate limited confidence in data analysis and inconsistent monitoring practices, PD will prioritize training in interpreting assessment reports, aligning instructional responses to data trends, and establishing consistent progress monitoring protocols. 2. Academic Achievement and Content Integration DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 266 of 292 A. Language-Integrated Content Instruction Data Findings and Trends: Classroom walkthroughs conducted between September and November 2025 found that only 47% of math and science lessons included explicit language objectives. Curriculum audits showed that content-area lesson plans frequently lacked aligned vocabulary and discourse supports. Spring 2025 state assessments showed a 22-point proficiency gap in math between ELLs and non-ELLs in Grades 4–5. PD Justification: Given limited language integration in content instruction and persistent achievement gaps, PD will focus on developing content-language alignment, academic vocabulary instruction, and discourse scaffolds in math and science classrooms. B. Differentiation and Instructional Scaffolding Data Findings and Trends: Collaborative planning surveys (October 2025) indicated that 69% of teachers reported difficulty differentiating instruction in mixed-proficiency classrooms. Literacy assessment data showed that only 35% of multilingual learners met expected reading growth benchmarks. Observation reports documented frequent use of whole-group instruction without tiered supports. PD Justification: Due to limited differentiation practices and low literacy growth rates, PD will emphasize tiered instruction, scaffolded reading strategies, and flexible grouping models to better meet diverse learner needs. C. ENL–Content Teacher Collaboration Data Findings and Trends: Planning logs from SY 2024–2025 showed that only 58% of ENL-content teams met consistently. Comparative analysis revealed that co-taught classrooms demonstrated a 15% higher language growth rate than isolated instructional settings. Teacher feedback indicated inconsistent co-planning structures. PD Justification: Because data demonstrates improved outcomes in collaborative settings, PD will prioritize co-planning protocols, shared lesson design, and role clarification for ENL and content teachers. 3. Social and Cultural Integration A. Building Inclusive Classroom Communities Data Findings and Trends: The 2025 School Climate Survey indicated that only 71% of students reported a sense of belonging, representing a decline from 74% in 2024. Guidance counselor logs documented increased peer integration challenges among newcomer students. Teacher reflections highlighted limited use of community-building routines. PD Justification: DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 267 of 292 In response to declining belonging indicators, PD will focus on classroom community-building strategies, inclusive routines, and culturally responsive relationship-building practices. B. Social-Emotional Learning and Trauma-Informed Practices Data Findings and Trends: Behavior and counseling data from Fall 2025 indicated that newcomer ELLs accounted for 38% of counseling referrals. Attendance records showed higher rates of absenteeism among students receiving referrals. Teacher surveys revealed that 61% of staff requested training in trauma-informed practices. PD Justification: Because data reflects elevated emotional support needs, PD will emphasize trauma-informed instruction, SEL integration, and classroom-based mental health supports. C. Community and Family Partnerships Data Findings and Trends: Parent interviews (Spring 2025) showed that fewer than 50% of families were aware of available community services. Referral data indicated that only 15% of eligible families accessed external supports. Family engagement attendance logs reflected low participation rates in support workshops. PD Justification: Given limited family access to external resources, PD will include training on family outreach strategies, community partnership development, and culturally responsive communication practices. Please specify how all teachers working in the Title III Immigrant program will receive ongoing professional development related to the Title III Immigrant program. Our Title III Immigrant Program ensures that all teachers and instructional staff supporting immigrant and newcomer English Language Learners receive ongoing, data-driven professional development aligned to the specific linguistic, academic, and social-emotional needs of their students. Professional learning opportunities are structured to build teacher expertise in language acquisition, culturally responsive instruction, and academic content integration, while fostering sustained collaboration across grade levels and content areas. 1. Needs Assessment and Targeting Comprehensive Needs Analysis: At the start of the 2025–2026 school year, the school leadership team conducted a Fall 2025 needs assessment through teacher surveys, classroom walkthroughs, and feedback from ENL team meetings. This process identified critical focus areas including: Consistent implementation of sheltered instruction (SIOP) practices across content areas. Enhanced use of formative assessment tools to track ELL progress. Stronger cross-curricular collaboration between ENL and general education teachers. Differentiated PD Pathways: Based on the assessment results, professional development tracks were designed for specific teacher groups: ENL Teachers: Advanced PD on data-informed planning, co-teaching models, and NYSESLAT-aligned formative assessment. ELA Teachers: Focused PD on integrating language objectives into literacy instruction and supporting multilingual writing. Content Area Teachers (Math, Science, Social Studies): Training on vocabulary scaffolding, language-rich questioning, and collaborative lesson design with ENL partners. New Teachers and Paraprofessionals: Foundational workshops on second language acquisition, classroom language supports, and strategies for promoting social-emotional belonging among immigrant students. 2. Accessibility and Delivery Formats Varied Learning Formats: To meet the diverse learning needs of staff, PD will be offered through multiple modalities between January and March 2026, coinciding with the Early Bird Program cycle: Workshops and Seminars: In-person, after-school sessions focused on practical classroom strategies and model lessons. Online Modules: Asynchronous courses through DOE-approved platforms (e.g., WeLearn, TeachHub) for flexibility and continuity. Instructional Coaching: One-on-one and small-group coaching cycles with the ENL Coordinator to provide individualized feedback and support. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 269 of 292 Professional Learning Communities (PLCs): Monthly PLC meetings where ENL and content teachers collaboratively plan lessons, analyze student data, and share effective practices. Scheduling and Participation: Sessions will take place after school and during designated PD days, ensuring no disruption to core instruction. Substitute coverage will be available when daytime workshops are scheduled. Attendance will be tracked electronically through the Title III Immigrant PD log maintained by the ENL Coordinator. 3. Monitoring and Evaluation Pre- and Post-Training Assessments: Teachers will complete pre- and post-assessments measuring knowledge of SIOP strategies, CR-SE practices, and ELL differentiation techniques. Feedback and Reflection: Participant surveys, focus groups, and coaching reflections will be collected after each session to evaluate PD impact and identify additional areas of need. Data Integration: Findings from teacher observations (Danielson components 1e, 3b, 3c, 3d) and EDAT subgroup performance reports will be reviewed to measure how PD participation translates into improved instructional practice and student outcomes. Ongoing Review: The ENL Coordinator and Assistant Principal will analyze PD data quarterly to adjust the professional learning plan as needed and ensure sustained growth. 4. Sustainability and Integration Budget and Funding: All PD activities for staff working in the Title III Immigrant Early Bird Program will be funded through Title III Immigrant allocations for SY 2025–2026. Teacher per-session compensation will remain within 10% of the total Title III Immigrant allocation. No external vendor costs are projected; all PD is school-based and internally facilitated. Integration with Schoolwide PD: Title III Immigrant professional learning is embedded into the school’s Comprehensive Educational Plan (CEP) and schoolwide PD calendar to ensure alignment with broader instructional goals. Collaboration with External Partners: The school will continue to collaborate with the DOE Division of Multilingual Learners (DML) and local community organizations to access supplemental training materials and cultural enrichment resources for teachers and students. 5. Rationale for Family Engagement Activities Describe the rationale for the family engagement activities to be provided: For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., reasons for topics, time activities will be offered), and Data sources (e.g., parent survey responses, in-house parent surveys, PTA/PA request) used to justify the need for activities. Rationale for Family Engagement Activities – Title III Immigrant Program (SY 2025–2026) Our Title III Immigrant Program’s family engagement activities for the 2025–2026 school year are grounded in a comprehensive analysis of qualitative and quantitative data, which clearly demonstrate the need for structured interventions and supports that strengthen communication, trust, and collaboration between immigrant families and the school community. The rationale for each category of activity is based on findings from multiple data sources, including parent and student feedback, attendance data, academic outcomes, and school climate measures. 1. Building Relationships and Trust Rationale: Qualitative Data: Parent surveys and interviews conducted during the Fall 2025 family needs assessment revealed that many newly arrived immigrant families experience feelings of isolation and uncertainty regarding school procedures, communication channels, and available support services. Quantitative Data: Attendance data from school events and Fall 2025 parent-teacher conferences showed that only 58% of newcomer families participated in at least one school-based event, suggesting a need to build stronger trust and communication pathways. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 279 of 292 Activities: Welcome events for newcomer families, parent-teacher conferences with interpreters, family resource centers, and optional home visits—all designed to establish personal connections, promote two-way communication, and increase parents’ comfort with the school environment. Time: Ongoing throughout the school year, with Welcome Breakfasts in January 2026 and additional outreach during conference periods and enrollment cycles. 2. Supporting Language Acquisition and Academic Achievement Rationale: Qualitative Data: Teacher feedback and ENL coordinator reports identified ongoing communication challenges between staff and families regarding student academic progress, homework expectations, and online learning platforms. Quantitative Data: Spring 2025 NYSESLAT results showed that 15% of ELLs in grades 2–5 remained at the Entering (EN) or Emerging (EM) levels, while parent feedback indicated limited understanding of how to support language learning at home. Activities: English language acquisition workshops, bilingual curriculum information sessions, study skills and homework support workshops, and ongoing translation and interpretation services to ensure families can effectively assist their children and access school communications. Time: Regularly scheduled during the January–March 2026 Early Bird Program period, with additional sessions offered during curriculum nights and parent-teacher conference weeks to maximize attendance. 3. Cultural Understanding and Community Building Rationale: Qualitative Data: Student interviews and small-group discussions revealed that some immigrant students felt culturally disconnected from school life, expressing a desire to see their heritage recognized and celebrated within the school community. Quantitative Data: Analysis of 2025 School Climate Survey results and disciplinary data showed a higher incidence of behavioral referrals among newcomer ELLs, suggesting potential cultural misunderstandings and a need for greater inclusion and representation. Activities: Cultural heritage celebrations, family story circles and bilingual book clubs, parent advisory councils, and partnerships with local community organizations. These activities promote cross-cultural appreciation, affirm students’ identities, and strengthen the partnership between home and school. Time: Implemented throughout the school year, with major cultural events in March 2026 and continuous participation in advisory councils and partnerships. Building Relationships and Trust: Welcome Potluck: Celebrate diversity and foster community through a shared meal where families bring dishes from their cultures. Family Fun Day: Organize games, activities, and performances showcasing different cultures, creating a fun and relaxed environment for families to connect. Coffee Chats with the Principal: Host informal sessions where families can ask questions, share concerns, and get to know the school's leadership. Parent-Teacher Conference Tips & Tricks: Workshop on effective communication strategies for conferences, preparing questions, and collaborating with teachers on student goals. Supporting Language Acquisition and Academic Achievement: Building Home Libraries: Workshop on creating a language-rich environment at home, selecting age-appropriate books, and engaging in shared reading activities. Decoding Report Cards: Interactive session explaining common terms and assessments on report cards, empowering parents to understand their child's progress. Technology Tools for Learning: Introduce families to educational apps, online resources, and digital platforms to support students' learning at home and in the classroom. College and Career Readiness: Panel discussion with college advisors and professionals from diverse fields to inspire and inform families about future educational and career pathways. Cultural Understanding and Community Building: Cultural Heritage Showcase: Students share traditional music, dance, stories, and food from their cultures, fostering understanding and appreciation of diverse backgrounds. Intergenerational Book Club: Choose a book with themes relevant to immigrant families and host discussions where parents, grandparents, and students can share their perspectives and experiences. Community Resource Fair: Invite local organizations and service providers to share information about resources available to immigrant families, including healthcare, legal aid, and cultural enrichment programs. Family Service Project: Organize a volunteer project where families come together to contribute to the school or community, promoting collaboration and civic engagement. Family Notification of Activities and Translation and Interpretation Supports Describe: How families will be notified of Title III Immigrant family engagement activities (e.g., phone calls, emails), How notification of activities will be translated into parent/guardians’ preferred language, and How interpretation supports will be provided at events. Our Title III Immigrant Program prioritizes accessible, multilingual, and proactive communication to ensure that all families of immigrant students are informed of and encouraged to participate in family engagement activities. Notification, translation, and interpretation supports are designed to eliminate barriers and guarantee that every family—regardless of language or literacy level—has equitable access to program information and events. 1. Family Notification of Activities Multilingual Communication Channels To reach all families effectively, the school will utilize multiple communication platforms and delivery methods: Printed Flyers and Brochures: Distributed through student backpacks, school lobbies, and community partner organizations, with translations into families’ preferred languages (Spanish, Arabic, Bengali, Chinese, etc.). School Website and Social Media: Event details, flyers, and registration links will be posted in multiple languages on the school’s official website and social media platforms. Schoolwide Announcements and Newsletters: Monthly newsletters will include a “Title III Immigrant Events” section, translated into priority languages and distributed digitally and in print. Community Boards: Multilingual postings displayed in high-traffic areas (main office, parent coordinator office, cafeteria) to ensure visibility for families without regular internet access. Direct, Personalized Communication Phone Calls and Voice Messages: The Parent Coordinator and bilingual staff will make direct calls to families in their home language to invite them to upcoming events. Email Notifications: Translated event invitations sent via DOE email distribution lists. Text Message Reminders (SMORE/SchoolMessenger): Short, translated reminders sent 24 hours before events to ensure accessibility and high attendance. In-Person Reminders: Teachers and ENL staff will promote upcoming family engagement sessions during dismissal and parent pick-up times to maintain personal connections. Frequency: Notifications will begin two weeks before each event and will be repeated through multiple channels (flyer, text, email, phone call) to ensure consistent outreach and equitable access. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 287 of 292 2. Translation of Family Notifications Translation Procedures: All family engagement notifications will be translated into parents’ preferred languages as indicated in the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) and updated in ATS. The school will utilize the NYC DOE Translation and Interpretation Unit (TIU) for written translations and verified templates. Translated materials will be reviewed by the ENL Coordinator prior to distribution to ensure accuracy and cultural appropriateness. Flyers and newsletters will prominently state, “Translation available in your home language upon request”, with contact information for the Parent Coordinator. Languages Provided: Translated materials will be available in the school’s top home languages, including Spanish, Arabic, Bengali, Chinese, Urdu, and Haitian Creole, with additional languages provided as needed based on current enrollment data. 3. Interpretation Supports at Family Engagement Events In-Person Interpretation: At each Title III Immigrant family engagement activity, trained bilingual staff or DOE-contracted interpreters will provide simultaneous or consecutive interpretation services. Each event will include bilingual signage directing families to available interpreters. Teachers and staff will wear language identifier badges to help families easily identify language support personnel. Virtual Interpretation: For any virtual or hybrid sessions, interpretation will be available through DOE-approved platforms (e.g., Zoom language channels or Google Meet translation support). Materials Provided During Events: All handouts, slide decks, and workshop resources will be distributed in both English and translated versions corresponding to attendees’ home languages. Visuals and bilingual glossaries will accompany presentations to increase accessibility and comprehension. Monitoring and Feedback: After each family engagement event, the Parent Coordinator and ENL Coordinator will collect feedback on translation and interpretation quality through short bilingual surveys to improve future services. 4. Accessibility and Equity Commitment Flexible Scheduling: Activities are scheduled at various times (morning and evening) to accommodate families’ work schedules. DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 288 of 292 Childcare Services: Available during select family workshops to reduce barriers for parents with young children. Community Collaboration: Flyers and translated resources will be shared with local community organizations and cultural centers to broaden outreach beyond the school. Maintaining Records Describe how your school will maintain documentation (e.g. agendas, attendance sheets) of family engagement activities and notifications to families. Our school will maintain comprehensive and organized documentation of all Title III Immigrant family engagement activities and family notifications to ensure full compliance with NYC DOE and federal grant requirements. All records will be securely stored in both digital and physical formats for transparency, audit readiness, and long-term reference. 1. Documentation to Be Maintained For each family engagement activity and notification, the following evidence will be collected and archived: Agendas outlining objectives, topics covered, and facilitators. Attendance Sheets with printed names, signatures, dates, and event titles. Flyers and Notifications sent to families (in English and translated languages). Interpretation and Translation Logs indicating which languages were supported at each event. Photographs and Presentation Slides (where applicable) to document participation and engagement. Post-Event Feedback Forms from families to assess satisfaction and areas for improvement. Parent Communication Logs from the ENL Coordinator and Parent Coordinator verifying calls, texts, and email outreach to families. All records will clearly label the event name, date, Title III funding designation, and staff responsible. 2. Storage and Organization A dedicated Title III Immigrant Shared Drive (Google Drive) will serve as the primary cloud-based repository. Files will be organized into folders for: Family Engagement Events (agendas, attendance, flyers) Family Notifications & Translations (communication logs, language access files) Professional Development Records Fiscal Documentation (OTPS & Per-Session) Physical copies of key documents (sign-in sheets, invoices, per-session forms) will be stored in the Title III binder DBN: 24Q014 School: P.S. 014 Fairview 289 of 292 housed in the main office. Retention Period: All documentation will be kept for a minimum of seven years in accordance with NYC DOE record- keeping and federal Title III Immigrant grant guidelines. 3. Roles and Responsibilities Assistant Principal (ENL/Bilingual Programs): Oversees program compliance, verifies record completeness, and ensures fiscal documentation is accurate and audit-ready. ENL Coordinator: Maintains digital and hard-copy records of all family engagement activities and notifications, uploads evidence to the shared drive, and submits monthly summaries to the Assistant Principal. Parent Coordinator: Documents family outreach, interpreters used, and communication methods; collects sign-ins and feedback forms at all events. ENL/Bilingual Teachers: Submit family engagement evidence (agendas, artifacts, participation notes) following each session. School Aide: Assists with scanning, labeling, and uploading documentation; supports tracking attendance and maintaining digital archives. 4. Compliance and Monitoring The Assistant Principal and ENL Coordinator will conduct quarterly audits of all Title III Immigrant folders to confirm that required materials are complete and properly labeled. Evidence will be reviewed prior to submission to the Division of Multilingual Learners (DML) during program monitoring cycles. By maintaining accurate, multilingual, and well-organized records, our school ensures full accountability and transparency for all Title III Immigrant family engagement initiatives—demonstrating a clear commitment to inclusive communication, parent involvement, and equitable access for every immigrant family.
